A PRINTED IDENTITY IN sec_arithmetic_critical.tex IS FALSE AS WRITTEN. It is an off-by-one, the
conclusion survives it, and it must still be fixed: it is printed with an equals sign, so as it
stands the paper asserts something untrue.

In the proof introducing the equilibrium mass and its tail, the paper defines

    p^e_j = \overline F(j-1) / \mu

and then prints

    \overline F^e(j) = \sum_{k>j} p^e_k = (1/\mu) \sum_{r>j} \overline F(r).

Substituting r = k-1 in the middle sum: k ranges over k >= j+1, so r ranges over r >= j. The exact
identity is therefore

    \overline F^e(j) = (1/\mu) \sum_{r >= j} \overline F(r),

with a lower limit of r >= j, not r > j. The omitted term is \overline F(j)/\mu, which is
O(j^{-alpha}) against a sum of order j^{1-alpha}/(alpha-1), so it is lower order and the Karamata
asymptotic and the final constant are unaffected. That is why this is a repair and not a
recalculation.

The same expression is printed a second time a few lines later, where discrete Karamata summation
is applied to supply the constant. Fix BOTH occurrences.

WHAT TO DO:
 1. Correct both printed identities to the r >= j form.
 2. Re-derive the Karamata step from the corrected expression and confirm in the text that the
    constant is unchanged. If the cleanest presentation is to state the exact identity with r >= j
    and then note that the j-th term is lower order before applying Karamata, do that - one short
    sentence, no new numbered result.
 3. Check the rest of that proof and the surrounding lemma for any other index-boundary slip of the
    same kind: every sum limit, every shifted-index substitution, every appeal to the renewal
    sequence u_j versus v_j indexing. Report what you checked and what you found, including
    "checked and correct" cases. This class of error travels in pairs.

DO NOT change any theorem statement, any hypothesis, any constant, the page budget, or the

## Instruction Priority

Superpowers skills override default system prompt behavior, but **user instructions always take precedence**:

1. **User's explicit instructions** (CLAUDE.md, GEMINI.md, AGENTS.md, direct requests) â€” highest priority
2. **Superpowers skills** â€” override default system behavior where they conflict

**In Claude Code:** Use the `Skill` tool. When you invoke a skill, its content is loaded and presented to youâ€”follow it directly. Never use the Read tool on skill files.

# Using Skills

## The Rule

**Invoke relevant or requested skills BEFORE any response or action.** Even a 1% chance a skill might apply means that you should invoke the skill to check. If an invoked skill turns out to be wrong for the situation, you don't need to use it.

# PDF Skill

## When to use
- Read or review PDF content where layout and visuals matter.
- Create PDFs programmatically with reliable formatting.
- Validate final rendering before delivery.

## Workflow
1. Prefer visual review: render PDF pages to PNGs and inspect them.
   - Use `pdftoppm` if available.
   - If unavailable, install Poppler or ask the user to review the output locally.
2. Use `reportlab` to generate PDFs when creating new documents.
3. Use `pdfplumber` (or `pypdf`) for text extraction and quick checks; do not rely on it for layout fidelity.
4. After each meaningful update, re-render pages and verify alignment, spacing, and legibility.

## Temp and output conventions
- Use `tmp/pdfs/` for intermediate files; delete when done.
- Write final artifacts under `output/pdf/` when working in this repo.
- Keep filenames stable and descriptive.

## Dependencies (install if missing)
Prefer `uv` for dependency management.

Python packages:
```
uv pip install reportlab pdfplumber pypdf
```
If `uv` is unavailable:
```
python3 -m pip install reportlab pdfplumber pypdf
```
System tools (for rendering):
```
# macOS (Homebrew)
brew install poppler

# Ubuntu/Debian
sudo apt-get install -y poppler-utils
```

If installation isn't possible in this environment, tell the user which dependency is missing and how to install it locally.

## Environment
No required environment variables.

## Rendering command
```
pdftoppm -png $INPUT_PDF $OUTPUT_PREFIX
```

## Quality expectations
- Maintain polished visual design: consistent typography, spacing, margins, and section hierarchy.
- Avoid rendering issues: clipped text, overlapping elements, broken tables, black squares, or unreadable glyphs.
- Charts, tables, and images must be sharp, aligned, and clearly labeled.
- Use ASCII hyphens only. Avoid U+2011 (non-breaking hyphen) and other Unicode dashes.
- Citations and references must be human-readable; never leave tool tokens or placeholder strings.

## Final checks
- Do not deliver until the latest PNG inspection shows zero visual or formatting defects.
- Confirm headers/footers, page numbering, and section transitions look polished.
- Keep intermediate files organized or remove them after final approval.

name: verification-before-completion

# Verification Before Completion

## Overview

Claiming work is complete without verification is dishonesty, not efficiency.

**Core principle:** Evidence before claims, always.

**Violating the letter of this rule is violating the spirit of this rule.**

## The Iron Law

```
NO COMPLETION CLAIMS WITHOUT FRESH VERIFICATION EVIDENCE
```

If you haven't run the verification command in this message, you cannot claim it passes.

## The Gate Function

```
BEFORE claiming any status or expressing satisfaction:

1. IDENTIFY: What command proves this claim?
2. RUN: Execute the FULL command (fresh, complete)
3. READ: Full output, check exit code, count failures
4. VERIFY: Does output confirm the claim?
   - If NO: State actual status with evidence
   - If YES: State claim WITH evidence
5. ONLY THEN: Make the claim

Skip any step = lying, not verifying
```

## Common Failures

| Claim | Requires | Not Sufficient |
|-------|----------|----------------|
| Tests pass | Test command output: 0 failures | Previous run, "should pass" |
| Linter clean | Linter output: 0 errors | Partial check, extrapolation |
| Build succeeds | Build command: exit 0 | Linter passing, logs look good |
| Bug fixed | Test original symptom: passes | Code changed, assumed fixed |
| Regression test works | Red-green cycle verified | Test passes once |
| Agent completed | VCS diff shows changes | Agent reports "success" |
| Requirements met | Line-by-line checklist | Tests passing |

## Red Flags - STOP

- Using "should", "probably", "seems to"
- Expressing satisfaction before verification ("Great!", "Perfect!", "Done!", etc.)
- About to commit/push/PR without verification
- Trusting agent success reports
- Relying on partial verification
- Thinking "just this once"
- Tired and wanting work over
- **ANY wording implying success without having run verification**

## Rationalization Prevention

| Excuse | Reality |
|--------|---------|
| "Should work now" | RUN the verification |
| "I'm confident" | Confidence â‰  evidence |
| "Just this once" | No exceptions |
| "Linter passed" | Linter â‰  compiler |
| "Agent said success" | Verify independently |
| "I'm tired" | Exhaustion â‰  excuse |
| "Partial check is enough" | Partial proves nothing |
| "Different words so rule doesn't apply" | Spirit over letter |

## Key Patterns

**Tests:**
```
âœ… [Run test command] [See: 34/34 pass] "All tests pass"
âŒ "Should pass now" / "Looks correct"
```

**Regression tests (TDD Red-Green):**
```
âœ… Write â†’ Run (pass) â†’ Revert fix â†’ Run (MUST FAIL) â†’ Restore â†’ Run (pass)
âŒ "I've written a regression test" (without red-green verification)
```

**Build:**
```
âœ… [Run build] [See: exit 0] "Build passes"
âŒ "Linter passed" (linter doesn't check compilation)
```

**Requirements:**
```
âœ… Re-read plan â†’ Create checklist â†’ Verify each â†’ Report gaps or completion
âŒ "Tests pass, phase complete"
```

**Agent delegation:**
```
âœ… Agent reports success â†’ Check VCS diff â†’ Verify changes â†’ Report actual state
âŒ Trust agent report
```

## Why This Matters

From 24 failure memories:
- your human partner said "I don't believe you" - trust broken
- Undefined functions shipped - would crash
- Missing requirements shipped - incomplete features
- Time wasted on false completion â†’ redirect â†’ rework
- Violates: "Honesty is a core value. If you lie, you'll be replaced."

## When To Apply

**ALWAYS before:**
- ANY variation of success/completion claims
- ANY expression of satisfaction
- ANY positive statement about work state
- Committing, PR creation, task completion
- Moving to next task
- Delegating to agents

**Rule applies to:**
- Exact phrases
- Paraphrases and synonyms
- Implications of success
- ANY communication suggesting completion/correctness

## The Bottom Line

**No shortcuts for verification.**

Run the command. Read the output. THEN claim the result.

This is non-negotiable.

name: systematic-debugging
description: Use when encountering any bug, test failure, or unexpected behavior, before proposing fixes

# Systematic Debugging

## Overview

Random fixes waste time and create new bugs. Quick patches mask underlying issues.

**Core principle:** ALWAYS find root cause before attempting fixes. Symptom fixes are failure.

**Violating the letter of this process is violating the spirit of debugging.**

## The Iron Law

```
NO FIXES WITHOUT ROOT CAUSE INVESTIGATION FIRST
```

If you haven't completed Phase 1, you cannot propose fixes.

## When to Use

Use for ANY technical issue:
- Test failures
- Bugs in production
- Unexpected behavior
- Performance problems
- Build failures
- Integration issues

**Use this ESPECIALLY when:**
- Under time pressure (emergencies make guessing tempting)
- "Just one quick fix" seems obvious
- You've already tried multiple fixes
- Previous fix didn't work
- You don't fully understand the issue

**Don't skip when:**
- Issue seems simple (simple bugs have root causes too)
- You're in a hurry (rushing guarantees rework)
- Manager wants it fixed NOW (systematic is faster than thrashing)

## The Four Phases

You MUST complete each phase before proceeding to the next.

### Phase 1: Root Cause Investigation

**BEFORE attempting ANY fix:**

1. **Read Error Messages Carefully**
   - Don't skip past errors or warnings
   - They often contain the exact solution
   - Read stack traces completely
   - Note line numbers, file paths, error codes

2. **Reproduce Consistently**
   - Can you trigger it reliably?
   - What are the exact steps?
   - Does it happen every time?
   - If not reproducible â†’ gather more data, don't guess

3. **Check Recent Changes**
   - What changed that could cause this?
   - Git diff, recent commits
   - New dependencies, config changes
   - Environmental differences

4. **Gather Evidence in Multi-Component Systems**

   **WHEN system has multiple components (CI â†’ build â†’ signing, API â†’ service â†’ database):**

   **BEFORE proposing fixes, add diagnostic instrumentation:**
   ```
   For EACH component boundary:
     - Log what data enters component
     - Log what data exits component
     - Verify environment/config propagation
     - Check state at each layer

   Run once to gather evidence showing WHERE it breaks
   THEN analyze evidence to identify failing component
   THEN investigate that specific component
   ```

   **Example (multi-layer system):**
   ```bash
   # Layer 1: Workflow
   echo "=== Secrets available in workflow: ==="
   echo "IDENTITY: ${IDENTITY:+SET}${IDENTITY:-UNSET}"

   # Layer 2: Build script
   echo "=== Env vars in build script: ==="
   env | grep IDENTITY || echo "IDENTITY not in environment"

   # Layer 3: Signing script
   echo "=== Keychain state: ==="
   security list-keychains
   security find-identity -v

   # Layer 4: Actual signing
   codesign --sign "$IDENTITY" --verbose=4 "$APP"
   ```

   **This reveals:** Which layer fails (secrets â†’ workflow âœ“, workflow â†’ build âœ—)

5. **Trace Data Flow**

   **WHEN error is deep in call stack:**

   See `root-cause-tracing.md` in this directory for the complete backward tracing technique.

   **Quick version:**
   - Where does bad value originate?
   - What called this with bad value?
   - Keep tracing up until you find the source
   - Fix at source, not at symptom

### Phase 2: Pattern Analysis

**Find the pattern before fixing:**

1. **Find Working Examples**
   - Locate similar working code in same codebase
   - What works that's similar to what's broken?

2. **Compare Against References**
   - If implementing pattern, read reference implementation COMPLETELY
   - Don't skim - read every line
   - Understand the pattern fully before applying

3. **Identify Differences**
   - What's different between working and broken?
   - List every difference, however small
   - Don't assume "that can't matter"

4. **Understand Dependencies**
   - What other components does this need?
   - What settings, config, environment?
   - What assumptions does it make?

### Phase 3: Hypothesis and Testing

**Scientific method:**

1. **Form Single Hypothesis**
   - State clearly: "I think X is the root cause because Y"
   - Write it down
   - Be specific, not vague

2. **Test Minimally**
   - Make the SMALLEST possible change to test hypothesis
   - One variable at a time
   - Don't fix multiple things at once

3. **Verify Before Continuing**
   - Did it work? Yes â†’ Phase 4
   - Didn't work? Form NEW hypothesis
   - DON'T add more fixes on top

4. **When You Don't Know**
   - Say "I don't understand X"
   - Don't pretend to know
   - Ask for help
   - Research more

### Phase 4: Implementation

**Fix the root cause, not the symptom:**

1. **Create Failing Test Case**
   - Simplest possible reproduction
   - Automated test if possible
   - One-off test script if no framework
   - MUST have before fixing
   - Use the `superpowers:test-driven-development` skill for writing proper failing tests

2. **Implement Single Fix**
   - Address the root cause identified
   - ONE change at a time
   - No "while I'm here" improvements
   - No bundled refactoring

3. **Verify Fix**
   - Test passes now?
   - No other tests broken?
   - Issue actually resolved?

4. **If Fix Doesn't Work**
   - STOP
   - Count: How many fixes have you tried?
   - If < 3: Return to Phase 1, re-analyze with new information
   - **If â‰¥ 3: STOP and question the architecture (step 5 below)**
   - DON'T attempt Fix #4 without architectural discussion

5. **If 3+ Fixes Failed: Question Architecture**

   **Pattern indicating architectural problem:**
   - Each fix reveals new shared state/coupling/problem in different place
   - Fixes require "massive refactoring" to implement
   - Each fix creates new symptoms elsewhere

   **STOP and question fundamentals:**
   - Is this pattern fundamentally sound?
   - Are we "sticking with it through sheer inertia"?
   - Should we refactor architecture vs. continue fixing symptoms?

   **Discuss with your human partner before attempting more fixes**

   This is NOT a failed hypothesis - this is a wrong architecture.

## Red Flags - STOP and Follow Process

If you catch yourself thinking:
- "Quick fix for now, investigate later"
- "Just try changing X and see if it works"
- "Add multiple changes, run tests"
- "Skip the test, I'll manually verify"
- "It's probably X, let me fix that"
- "I don't fully understand but this might work"
- "Pattern says X but I'll adapt it differently"
- "Here are the main problems: [lists fixes without investigation]"
- Proposing solutions before tracing data flow
- **"One more fix attempt" (when already tried 2+)**
- **Each fix reveals new problem in different place**

**ALL of these mean: STOP. Return to Phase 1.**

**If 3+ fixes failed:** Question the architecture (see Phase 4.5)

## your human partner's Signals You're Doing It Wrong

**Watch for these redirections:**
- "Is that not happening?" - You assumed without verifying
- "Will it show us...?" - You should have added evidence gathering
- "Stop guessing" - You're proposing fixes without understanding
- "Ultrathink this" - Question fundamentals, not just symptoms
- "We're stuck?" (frustrated) - Your approach isn't working

**When you see these:** STOP. Return to Phase 1.

## Common Rationalizations

| Excuse | Reality |
|--------|---------|
| "Issue is simple, don't need process" | Simple issues have root causes too. Process is fast for simple bugs. |
| "Emergency, no time for process" | Systematic debugging is FASTER than guess-and-check thrashing. |
| "Just try this first, then investigate" | First fix sets the pattern. Do it right from the start. |
| "I'll write test after confirming fix works" | Untested fixes don't stick. Test first proves it. |
| "Multiple fixes at once saves time" | Can't isolate what worked. Causes new bugs. |
| "Reference too long, I'll adapt the pattern" | Partial understanding guarantees bugs. Read it completely. |
| "I see the problem, let me fix it" | Seeing symptoms â‰  understanding root cause. |
| "One more fix attempt" (after 2+ failures) | 3+ failures = architectural problem. Question pattern, don't fix again. |

## Quick Reference

| Phase | Key Activities | Success Criteria |
|-------|---------------|------------------|
| **1. Root Cause** | Read errors, reproduce, check changes, gather evidence | Understand WHAT and WHY |
| **2. Pattern** | Find working examples, compare | Identify differences |
| **3. Hypothesis** | Form theory, test minimally | Confirmed or new hypothesis |
| **4. Implementation** | Create test, fix, verify | Bug resolved, tests pass |

## When Process Reveals "No Root Cause"

If systematic investigation reveals issue is truly environmental, timing-dependent, or external:

1. You've completed the process
2. Document what you investigated
3. Implement appropriate handling (retry, timeout, error message)
4. Add monitoring/logging for future investigation

**But:** 95% of "no root cause" cases are incomplete investigation.

## Supporting Techniques

These techniques are part of systematic debugging and available in this directory:

- **`root-cause-tracing.md`** - Trace bugs backward through call stack to find original trigger
- **`defense-in-depth.md`** - Add validation at multiple layers after finding root cause
- **`condition-based-waiting.md`** - Replace arbitrary timeouts with condition polling

**Related skills:**
- **superpowers:test-driven-development** - For creating failing test case (Phase 4, Step 1)
- **superpowers:verification-before-completion** - Verify fix worked before claiming success

## Real-World Impact

From debugging sessions:
- Systematic approach: 15-30 minutes to fix
- Random fixes approach: 2-3 hours of thrashing
- First-time fix rate: 95% vs 40%
- New bugs introduced: Near zero vs common

name: test-driven-development
description: Use when implementing any feature or bugfix, before writing implementation code
---

# Test-Driven Development (TDD)

## Overview

Write the test first. Watch it fail. Write minimal code to pass.

**Core principle:** If you didn't watch the test fail, you don't know if it tests the right thing.

**Violating the letter of the rules is violating the spirit of the rules.**

## When to Use

**Always:**
- New features
- Bug fixes
- Refactoring
- Behavior changes

**Exceptions (ask your human partner):**
- Throwaway prototypes
- Generated code
- Configuration files

Thinking "skip TDD just this once"? Stop. That's rationalization.

## The Iron Law

```
NO PRODUCTION CODE WITHOUT A FAILING TEST FIRST
```

Write code before the test? Delete it. Start over.

**No exceptions:**
- Don't keep it as "reference"
- Don't "adapt" it while writing tests
- Don't look at it
- Delete means delete

Implement fresh from tests. Period.

## Red-Green-Refactor

```dot
digraph tdd_cycle {
    rankdir=LR;
    red [label="RED\nWrite failing test", shape=box, style=filled, fillcolor="#ffcccc"];
    verify_red [label="Verify fails\ncorrectly", shape=diamond];
    green [label="GREEN\nMinimal code", shape=box, style=filled, fillcolor="#ccffcc"];
    verify_green [label="Verify passes\nAll green", shape=diamond];
    refactor [label="REFACTOR\nClean up", shape=box, style=filled, fillcolor="#ccccff"];
    next [label="Next", shape=ellipse];

    red -> verify_red;
    verify_red -> green [label="yes"];
    verify_red -> red [label="wrong\nfailure"];
    green -> verify_green;
    verify_green -> refactor [label="yes"];
    verify_green -> green [label="no"];
    refactor -> verify_green [label="stay\ngreen"];
    verify_green -> next;
    next -> red;
}
```

### RED - Write Failing Test

Write one minimal test showing what should happen.

<Good>
```typescript
test('retries failed operations 3 times', async () => {
  let attempts = 0;
  const operation = () => {
    attempts++;
    if (attempts < 3) throw new Error('fail');
    return 'success';
  };

  const result = await retryOperation(operation);

  expect(result).toBe('success');
  expect(attempts).toBe(3);
});
```
Clear name, tests real behavior, one thing
</Good>

<Bad>
```typescript
test('retry works', async () => {
  const mock = jest.fn()
    .mockRejectedValueOnce(new Error())
    .mockRejectedValueOnce(new Error())
    .mockResolvedValueOnce('success');
  await retryOperation(mock);
  expect(mock).toHaveBeenCalledTimes(3);
});
```
Vague name, tests mock not code
</Bad>

**Requirements:**
- One behavior
- Clear name
- Real code (no mocks unless unavoidable)

### Verify RED - Watch It Fail

**MANDATORY. Never skip.**

```bash
npm test path/to/test.test.ts
```

Confirm:
- Test fails (not errors)
- Failure message is expected
- Fails because feature missing (not typos)

**Test passes?** You're testing existing behavior. Fix test.

**Test errors?** Fix error, re-run until it fails correctly.

### GREEN - Minimal Code

Write simplest code to pass the test.

<Good>
```typescript
async function retryOperation<T>(fn: () => Promise<T>): Promise<T> {
  for (let i = 0; i < 3; i++) {
    try {
      return await fn();
    } catch (e) {
      if (i === 2) throw e;
    }
  }
  throw new Error('unreachable');
}
```
Just enough to pass
</Good>

<Bad>
```typescript
async function retryOperation<T>(
  fn: () => Promise<T>,
  options?: {
    maxRetries?: number;
    backoff?: 'linear' | 'exponential';
    onRetry?: (attempt: number) => void;
  }
): Promise<T> {
  // YAGNI
}
```
Over-engineered
</Bad>

Don't add features, refactor other code, or "improve" beyond the test.

### Verify GREEN - Watch It Pass

**MANDATORY.**

```bash
npm test path/to/test.test.ts
```

Confirm:
- Test passes
- Other tests still pass
- Output pristine (no errors, warnings)

**Test fails?** Fix code, not test.

**Other tests fail?** Fix now.

### REFACTOR - Clean Up

After green only:
- Remove duplication
- Improve names
- Extract helpers

Keep tests green. Don't add behavior.

### Repeat

Next failing test for next feature.

## Good Tests

| Quality | Good | Bad |
|---------|------|-----|
| **Minimal** | One thing. "and" in name? Split it. | `test('validates email and domain and whitespace')` |
| **Clear** | Name describes behavior | `test('test1')` |
| **Shows intent** | Demonstrates desired API | Obscures what code should do |

## Why Order Matters

**"I'll write tests after to verify it works"**

Tests written after code pass immediately. Passing immediately proves nothing:
- Might test wrong thing
- Might test implementation, not behavior
- Might miss edge cases you forgot
- You never saw it catch the bug

Test-first forces you to see the test fail, proving it actually tests something.

**"I already manually tested all the edge cases"**

Manual testing is ad-hoc. You think you tested everything but:
- No record of what you tested
- Can't re-run when code changes
- Easy to forget cases under pressure
- "It worked when I tried it" â‰  comprehensive

Automated tests are systematic. They run the same way every time.

**"Deleting X hours of work is wasteful"**

Sunk cost fallacy. The time is already gone. Your choice now:
- Delete and rewrite with TDD (X more hours, high confidence)
- Keep it and add tests after (30 min, low confidence, likely bugs)

The "waste" is keeping code you can't trust. Working code without real tests is technical debt.

**"TDD is dogmatic, being pragmatic means adapting"**

TDD IS pragmatic:
- Finds bugs before commit (faster than debugging after)
- Prevents regressions (tests catch breaks immediately)
- Documents behavior (tests show how to use code)
- Enables refactoring (change freely, tests catch breaks)

"Pragmatic" shortcuts = debugging in production = slower.

**"Tests after achieve the same goals - it's spirit not ritual"**

No. Tests-after answer "What does this do?" Tests-first answer "What should this do?"

Tests-after are biased by your implementation. You test what you built, not what's required. You verify remembered edge cases, not discovered ones.

Tests-first force edge case discovery before implementing. Tests-after verify you remembered everything (you didn't).

30 minutes of tests after â‰  TDD. You get coverage, lose proof tests work.

## Common Rationalizations

| Excuse | Reality |
|--------|---------|
| "Too simple to test" | Simple code breaks. Test takes 30 seconds. |
| "I'll test after" | Tests passing immediately prove nothing. |
| "Tests after achieve same goals" | Tests-after = "what does this do?" Tests-first = "what should this do?" |
| "Already manually tested" | Ad-hoc â‰  systematic. No record, can't re-run. |
| "Deleting X hours is wasteful" | Sunk cost fallacy. Keeping unverified code is technical debt. |
| "Keep as reference, write tests first" | You'll adapt it. That's testing after. Delete means delete. |
| "Need to explore first" | Fine. Throw away exploration, start with TDD. |
| "Test hard = design unclear" | Listen to test. Hard to test = hard to use. |
| "TDD will slow me down" | TDD faster than debugging. Pragmatic = test-first. |
| "Manual test faster" | Manual doesn't prove edge cases. You'll re-test every change. |
| "Existing code has no tests" | You're improving it. Add tests for existing code. |

## Red Flags - STOP and Start Over

- Code before test
- Test after implementation
- Test passes immediately
- Can't explain why test failed
- Tests added "later"
- Rationalizing "just this once"
- "I already manually tested it"
- "Tests after achieve the same purpose"
- "It's about spirit not ritual"
- "Keep as reference" or "adapt existing code"
- "Already spent X hours, deleting is wasteful"
- "TDD is dogmatic, I'm being pragmatic"
- "This is different because..."

**All of these mean: Delete code. Start over with TDD.**

## Example: Bug Fix

**Bug:** Empty email accepted

**RED**
```typescript
test('rejects empty email', async () => {
  const result = await submitForm({ email: '' });
  expect(result.error).toBe('Email required');
});
```

**Verify RED**
```bash
$ npm test
FAIL: expected 'Email required', got undefined
```

**GREEN**
```typescript
function submitForm(data: FormData) {
  if (!data.email?.trim()) {
    return { error: 'Email required' };
  }
  // ...
}
```

**Verify GREEN**
```bash
$ npm test
PASS
```

**REFACTOR**
Extract validation for multiple fields if needed.

## Verification Checklist

Before marking work complete:

- [ ] Every new function/method has a test
- [ ] Watched each test fail before implementing
- [ ] Each test failed for expected reason (feature missing, not typo)
- [ ] Wrote minimal code to pass each test
- [ ] All tests pass
- [ ] Output pristine (no errors, warnings)
- [ ] Tests use real code (mocks only if unavoidable)
- [ ] Edge cases and errors covered

Can't check all boxes? You skipped TDD. Start over.

## When Stuck

| Problem | Solution |
|---------|----------|
| Don't know how to test | Write wished-for API. Write assertion first. Ask your human partner. |
| Test too complicated | Design too complicated. Simplify interface. |
| Must mock everything | Code too coupled. Use dependency injection. |
| Test setup huge | Extract helpers. Still complex? Simplify design. |

## Debugging Integration

Bug found? Write failing test reproducing it. Follow TDD cycle. Test proves fix and prevents regression.

Never fix bugs without a test.

## Testing Anti-Patterns

When adding mocks or test utilities, read @testing-anti-patterns.md to avoid common pitfalls:
- Testing mock behavior instead of real behavior
- Adding test-only methods to production classes
- Mocking without understanding dependencies

## Final Rule

```
Production code â†’ test exists and failed first
Otherwise â†’ not TDD
```

No exceptions without your human partner's permission.

80-\begin{lemma}[From the local denominator layer to the cost tail]
81-\label{lem:critical-tail-summation}
82-At \(s=\sigma_0\), the context series defining \(b_C\) is absolutely
83-convergent and
84-\[
85- b_C=2\left(\frac{\zeta(\sigma_0-1)}{\zeta(\sigma_0)}\right)^2=8.
86-\]
87-If \(\alpha=\sigma_0-1\), then
88-\begin{equation}
89- \overline F_C(x):=\Pr\{C>x\}
--
100-every \(s>2\), so their product is the absolutely convergent double series
101-in \eqref{eq:critical-tail-constants}.  Their product is \(2\rho_s^2\).
102-At the critical point \(\rho_{\sigma_0}=2\), which proves \(b_C=8\).
103-
104-By \Cref{thm:arithmetic-denominator-layer}, with \(d\) denoting the
105-negative-continuant depth,
106-\[
107- \Pr\{C=2d+1\}=b_{2d+1}(\sigma_0)
108- =b_Cd^{-\sigma_0}(1+o(1)).
109-\]
110-For real \(x\), the inequality \(2d+1>x\) is equivalent to
111-\(d>(x-1)/2\).  The integral comparison for a power sequence, or the
112:discrete Karamata theorem, gives
113-\[
114- \sum_{d>y}d^{-\sigma_0}
115- \sim\int_y^\infty t^{-\sigma_0}\,dt
116- =\frac{y^{1-\sigma_0}}{\sigma_0-1}.
117-\]
118-The same conclusion holds after multiplication by a sequence tending to
119-\(b_C\): for any \(\varepsilon>0\), all sufficiently large summands lie
120-between \((b_C-\varepsilon)d^{-\sigma_0}\) and
121-\((b_C+\varepsilon)d^{-\sigma_0}\), and the finitely many omitted terms do
122-not enter a tail.  Therefore
123-\begin{align*}
124- \overline F_C(x)
125- &=\sum_{d>(x-1)/2}b_{2d+1}(\sigma_0)\\
126- &\sim\frac{b_C}{\sigma_0-1}
127-       \left(\frac{x-1}{2}\right)^{1-\sigma_0}
128- =\frac{2^\alpha b_C}{\alpha}x^{-\alpha}.
129-\end{align*}
130-This is \eqref{eq:tail-summation-expanded}, and the last constant is
131-\(K_C=2^{\sigma_0+2}/(\sigma_0-1)\).
132-\end{proof}

134:\begin{lemma}[Second-order lattice renewal input]
135:\label{lem:second-order-lattice-renewal}
136-Let \((p_j)_{j\ge1}\) be a probability mass function on the positive
137-integers.  Suppose that
138-\begin{enumerate}
139: \item \(\gcd\{j:p_j>0\}=1\), so the renewal law is aperiodic of span one;
140- \item \(\mu=\sum_{j\ge1}jp_j<\infty\);
141- \item its tail \(\overline F(j)=\sum_{k>j}p_k\) satisfies
142- \(\overline F(j)\sim Kj^{-\alpha}\), where \(K>0\) and
143- \(\alpha>1\).
144-\end{enumerate}
145-Define \(v_0=1\) and
146-\(v_j=\sum_{k=1}^jp_kv_{j-k}\).  Then
147-\begin{equation}
148- v_j=\frac1\mu+\frac{K}{\mu^2(\alpha-1)}j^{1-\alpha}
149-      +o(j^{1-\alpha}).
150: \label{eq:second-order-renewal-input}
151-\end{equation}
152-\end{lemma}
153-
154-\begin{proof}
155-Introduce the equilibrium mass and its tail
156-\[
157- p^{\rm e}_j=\frac{\overline F(j-1)}\mu,
158- \qquad
159- \overline F^{\rm e}(j)
160- =\sum_{k>j}p^{\rm e}_k
161- =\frac1\mu\sum_{r>j}\overline F(r).
162-\]
163:The second-order assertion used here is the first displayed renewal
164-asymptotic in Omey and Van Gulck
165-\cite[Sec.~3.2, first assertion following Eq.~(8)]{OmeyVanGulck2015}:
166-under the three hypotheses above,
167-\begin{equation}
168- v_j-\frac1\mu\sim\frac{\overline F^{\rm e}(j)}\mu.
169- \label{eq:omey-vangulck-equilibrium-form}
170-\end{equation}
171-Their paper states the result for a tail regularly varying with index
172-\(-\alpha\), \(\alpha>1\); their \(u_n\), \(\mu\), and
173-\(\overline F_n\) are respectively \(v_j\), \(\mu\), and
174:\(\overline F(j)\) here.  Discrete Karamata summation supplies the constant:
175-\[
176- \overline F^{\rm e}(j)
177- =\frac1\mu\sum_{r>j}\overline F(r)
178- \sim\frac{K}{\mu(\alpha-1)}j^{1-\alpha}.
179-\]
180-Substitution in \eqref{eq:omey-vangulck-equilibrium-form} gives
181:\eqref{eq:second-order-renewal-input}.  Thus neither the power nor its
182-coefficient is hidden in the citation.
183-\end{proof}

200-\]
201-so this is a probability mass function.  Every cost is a positive odd
202-integer.  More particularly, the negative continued fractions
203-\(1/2=[0;2]_-\) and \(1/3=[0;3]_-\) have
204-\[
205- c(1/2)=2(2-1)+1=3,\qquad
206- c(1/3)=2(3-1)+1=5,
207-\]

209-\(3^{-\sigma_0}>0\).  Hence
210-\[
211- \gcd\{j:p_j>0\}\mid\gcd(3,5)=1.
212-\]
213:The renewal span is therefore one.  Equivalently, sums of threes and fives
214:contain every integer from eight onward, so the renewal epochs have no
215-nontrivial period.  The fact that each individual increment is odd does not
216-create a period, because the number of increments is not fixed.
217-

221-\[
222- \mu_C\ll\sum_{q\ge2}q^{1-\sigma_0}(\log q)^2<\infty
223-\]
224-because \(\sigma_0>2\).  Thus the mean hypothesis is verified directly
225-against the letter law, not inferred from its tail alone.
226-
227-Third, \(\rho_s=\zeta(s-1)/\zeta(s)\) decreases from infinity to one on

230-\(\rho_{\sigma_0}=2\) therefore puts \(2<\sigma_0<3\), hence
231-\(1<\alpha<2\).  Finally, \Cref{lem:critical-tail-summation} gives
232-\(\overline F_C(j)\sim K_Cj^{-\alpha}\).  All three hypotheses of
233:\Cref{lem:second-order-lattice-renewal} now hold.  Its conclusion is
234-\[

236- +\frac{K_C}{\mu_C^2(\sigma_0-2)}j^{2-\sigma_0}
237- +o(j^{2-\sigma_0}),
238-\]
239-as claimed.
240-\end{proof}

340-\begin{corollary}[Critical asymptotic for the classical Fibonacci partition function]
341-\label{cor:classical-fibonacci-critical-asymptotic}
342-Let \(R(N)\) count the representations of \(N\) as a sum of distinct
343-members of \(F_2,F_3,\ldots\), as in
344-\eqref{eq:fibonacci-partition-function}.  Then
345-\begin{align}
346- &\sum_{F_{m+1}-1\le N<F_{m+2}-1}R(N)^{-\sigma_0}
347- \notag\\
348- &\qquad=\frac{2m}{\mu_C}
349- +\frac{2K_C}{\mu_C^2(\sigma_0-2)(3-\sigma_0)}
350-  m^{3-\sigma_0}+o(m^{3-\sigma_0}).
351- \label{eq:classical-fibonacci-critical-asymptotic}
352-\end{align}
353:Equivalently, relative to the known linear renewal scale,
354-\begin{equation}
355- \sum_{N\in\mathcal I_m}R(N)^{-\sigma_0}
356- =\frac{2m}{\mu_C}\left[
357- 1+\frac{K_C}{\mu_C(\sigma_0-2)(3-\sigma_0)}
358- m^{2-\sigma_0}+o(m^{2-\sigma_0})\right].
359- \label{eq:relative-fibonacci-critical-correction}
360-\end{equation}
361-The exponent \(3-\sigma_0\) lies strictly between zero and one.  Hence the
362-second term diverges on the absolute partition-function scale, is smaller
363-than the leading linear term, and cannot be absorbed into a bounded endpoint
364-correction.
365-\end{corollary}
366-
367-\begin{proof}
368-The interval in \eqref{eq:classical-fibonacci-critical-asymptotic} is
369-exactly \(\mathcal I_m\), so the left side is
370-\(Z_m^R(-\sigma_0)\).  Equation
371-\eqref{eq:standard-layer-critical-second-order} proves the first expansion.
372-Factoring out \(2m/\mu_C\) gives
373-\eqref{eq:relative-fibonacci-critical-correction}.  The inequalities
374-\(2<\sigma_0<3\), already verified in
375:\Cref{prop:critical-renewal-hypotheses}, give the stated scale comparison.
376-\end{proof}

1:\section{The arithmetic critical law}\label{sec:arithmetic-critical}
2-
3:\begin{theorem}[Arithmetic critical stable renewal]
4:\label{thm:arithmetic-critical-stable-renewal}
5-Put \(\alpha=\sigma_0-1\in(1,2)\), and under the critical letter law put
6-\[
7- \Pr\{(C,H)=(c(p/q),\log q)\}=q^{-\sigma_0}
8- \qquad(p/q\in\mathcal A).
9-\]
10-Then \(\Pr\{C=2d+1\}=b_{2d+1}(\sigma_0)\), and the constants
11-\begin{equation}
12- \begin{aligned}
13- b_C&:=
14- \sum_{\mathbf u\in\mathcal W_{\rm L}}
15- \sum_{\mathbf v\in\mathcal W_{\rm R}}
16- \{\mathcal K(\mathbf u)\mathcal K(\mathbf v)\}^{-\sigma_0}
--
24-and
25-\begin{align}
26- b_{2d+1}(\sigma_0)&\sim b_Cd^{-\sigma_0},
27- &&d\to\infty,
28- \label{eq:critical-letter-local-tail}\\
29- \Pr\{C>x\}
30- &=\sum_{q\ge2}q^{-\sigma_0}
31-   \card{\{1\le p<q:(p,q)=1,\ c(p/q)>x\}}\notag\\
32- &\sim K_Cx^{1-\sigma_0},
33- &&x\to\infty.
34- \label{eq:critical-letter-tail}
35-\end{align}
36:If \(u_j=u_j(\sigma_0)\) is the renewal sequence in
37:\eqref{eq:weighted-renewal-coefficients}, then
38-\begin{equation}
39- u_j=\frac1{\mu_C}
40- +\frac{K_C}{\mu_C^2(\sigma_0-2)}j^{2-\sigma_0}
41- +o(j^{2-\sigma_0}).
42: \label{eq:critical-renewal-second-order}
43-\end{equation}
44-Consequently,
45-\begin{equation}
46- Z_m^R(-\sigma_0)
47- =\frac{2m}{\mu_C}
48- +\frac{2K_C}{\mu_C^2(\sigma_0-2)(3-\sigma_0)}
49-   m^{3-\sigma_0}+o(m^{3-\sigma_0}).
50- \label{eq:standard-layer-critical-second-order}
51-\end{equation}
52-Finally, \(C\) belongs to the domain of attraction of a spectrally positive
53-\(\alpha\)-stable law.  More precisely, if \(a_n>0\) is chosen so that
54-\(n\Pr\{C>a_n\}\to1\), then for independent copies \(C_1,C_2,\ldots\),
--
65- \label{eq:centered-stable-characteristic-exponent}
66-\end{equation}
67-In particular, the explicit choice
68-\begin{equation}
69- a_n=(K_Cn)^{1/\alpha}
70- =\left(\frac{2^{\sigma_0+2}}{\sigma_0-1}n\right)^{1/(\sigma_0-1)}
71- \label{eq:explicit-critical-stable-normalization}
72-\end{equation}
73-satisfies the required tail normalization.
74-\end{theorem}
75-
76-The proof is separated into the arithmetic tail calculation, the lattice
77:renewal input, and the one-sided stable criterion.  This also fixes the
78-normalizations at each transfer.
79-
80-\begin{lemma}[From the local denominator layer to the cost tail]
81-\label{lem:critical-tail-summation}
82-At \(s=\sigma_0\), the context series defining \(b_C\) is absolutely
83-convergent and
84-\[
85- b_C=2\left(\frac{\zeta(\sigma_0-1)}{\zeta(\sigma_0)}\right)^2=8.
86-\]
87-If \(\alpha=\sigma_0-1\), then
88-\begin{equation}
89- \overline F_C(x):=\Pr\{C>x\}
--
100-every \(s>2\), so their product is the absolutely convergent double series
101-in \eqref{eq:critical-tail-constants}.  Their product is \(2\rho_s^2\).
102-At the critical point \(\rho_{\sigma_0}=2\), which proves \(b_C=8\).
103-
104-By \Cref{thm:arithmetic-denominator-layer}, with \(d\) denoting the
105-negative-continuant depth,
106-\[
107- \Pr\{C=2d+1\}=b_{2d+1}(\sigma_0)
108- =b_Cd^{-\sigma_0}(1+o(1)).
109-\]
110-For real \(x\), the inequality \(2d+1>x\) is equivalent to
111-\(d>(x-1)/2\).  The integral comparison for a power sequence, or the
112:discrete Karamata theorem, gives
113-\[
114- \sum_{d>y}d^{-\sigma_0}
115- \sim\int_y^\infty t^{-\sigma_0}\,dt
116- =\frac{y^{1-\sigma_0}}{\sigma_0-1}.
117-\]
118-The same conclusion holds after multiplication by a sequence tending to
119-\(b_C\): for any \(\varepsilon>0\), all sufficiently large summands lie
120-between \((b_C-\varepsilon)d^{-\sigma_0}\) and
121-\((b_C+\varepsilon)d^{-\sigma_0}\), and the finitely many omitted terms do
122-not enter a tail.  Therefore
123-\begin{align*}
124- \overline F_C(x)
125- &=\sum_{d>(x-1)/2}b_{2d+1}(\sigma_0)\\
126- &\sim\frac{b_C}{\sigma_0-1}
127-       \left(\frac{x-1}{2}\right)^{1-\sigma_0}
128- =\frac{2^\alpha b_C}{\alpha}x^{-\alpha}.
129-\end{align*}
130-This is \eqref{eq:tail-summation-expanded}, and the last constant is
131-\(K_C=2^{\sigma_0+2}/(\sigma_0-1)\).
132-\end{proof}
133-
134:\begin{lemma}[Second-order lattice renewal input]
135:\label{lem:second-order-lattice-renewal}
136-Let \((p_j)_{j\ge1}\) be a probability mass function on the positive
137-integers.  Suppose that
138-\begin{enumerate}
139: \item \(\gcd\{j:p_j>0\}=1\), so the renewal law is aperiodic of span one;
140- \item \(\mu=\sum_{j\ge1}jp_j<\infty\);
141- \item its tail \(\overline F(j)=\sum_{k>j}p_k\) satisfies
142- \(\overline F(j)\sim Kj^{-\alpha}\), where \(K>0\) and
143- \(\alpha>1\).
144-\end{enumerate}
145-Define \(v_0=1\) and
146-\(v_j=\sum_{k=1}^jp_kv_{j-k}\).  Then
147-\begin{equation}
148- v_j=\frac1\mu+\frac{K}{\mu^2(\alpha-1)}j^{1-\alpha}
149-      +o(j^{1-\alpha}).
150: \label{eq:second-order-renewal-input}
151-\end{equation}
152-\end{lemma}
153-
154-\begin{proof}
155-Introduce the equilibrium mass and its tail
156-\[
157- p^{\rm e}_j=\frac{\overline F(j-1)}\mu,
158- \qquad
159- \overline F^{\rm e}(j)
160- =\sum_{k>j}p^{\rm e}_k
161- =\frac1\mu\sum_{r>j}\overline F(r).
162-\]
163:The second-order assertion used here is the first displayed renewal
164-asymptotic in Omey and Van Gulck
165-\cite[Sec.~3.2, first assertion following Eq.~(8)]{OmeyVanGulck2015}:
166-under the three hypotheses above,
167-\begin{equation}
168- v_j-\frac1\mu\sim\frac{\overline F^{\rm e}(j)}\mu.
169- \label{eq:omey-vangulck-equilibrium-form}
170-\end{equation}
171-Their paper states the result for a tail regularly varying with index
172-\(-\alpha\), \(\alpha>1\); their \(u_n\), \(\mu\), and
173-\(\overline F_n\) are respectively \(v_j\), \(\mu\), and
174:\(\overline F(j)\) here.  Discrete Karamata summation supplies the constant:
175-\[
176- \overline F^{\rm e}(j)
177- =\frac1\mu\sum_{r>j}\overline F(r)
178- \sim\frac{K}{\mu(\alpha-1)}j^{1-\alpha}.
179-\]
180-Substitution in \eqref{eq:omey-vangulck-equilibrium-form} gives
181:\eqref{eq:second-order-renewal-input}.  Thus neither the power nor its
182-coefficient is hidden in the citation.
183-\end{proof}
184-
185-\begin{proposition}[Verification for the critical letter law]
186:\label{prop:critical-renewal-hypotheses}
187-The mass function \(p_j=b_j(\sigma_0)\) satisfies every hypothesis of
188:\Cref{lem:second-order-lattice-renewal}, with
189-\[
190- \mu=\mu_C,\qquad \alpha=\sigma_0-1\in(1,2),\qquad K=K_C.
191-\]
192:Consequently \eqref{eq:critical-renewal-second-order} holds.
193-\end{proposition}
194-
195-\begin{proof}
196:First, \eqref{eq:weighted-renewal-at-one} and
197:\eqref{eq:critical-renewal-normalization} give
198-\[
199- \sum_{j\ge1}p_j=B_{\sigma_0}(1)=1,
200-\]
201-so this is a probability mass function.  Every cost is a positive odd
202-integer.  More particularly, the negative continued fractions
203-\(1/2=[0;2]_-\) and \(1/3=[0;3]_-\) have
204-\[
205- c(1/2)=2(2-1)+1=3,\qquad
206- c(1/3)=2(3-1)+1=5,
207-\]
208-and their masses are \(2^{-\sigma_0}>0\) and
209-\(3^{-\sigma_0}>0\).  Hence
210-\[
211- \gcd\{j:p_j>0\}\mid\gcd(3,5)=1.
212-\]
213:The renewal span is therefore one.  Equivalently, sums of threes and fives
214:contain every integer from eight onward, so the renewal epochs have no
215-nontrivial period.  The fact that each individual increment is odd does not
216-create a period, because the number of increments is not fixed.
217-
218-Second, the mean is exactly the series in
219-\eqref{eq:critical-cost-mean}.  The Panov--Liehl estimate recorded after
220-that equation gives
221-\[
222- \mu_C\ll\sum_{q\ge2}q^{1-\sigma_0}(\log q)^2<\infty
223-\]
224-because \(\sigma_0>2\).  Thus the mean hypothesis is verified directly
225-against the letter law, not inferred from its tail alone.
226-
227-Third, \(\rho_s=\zeta(s-1)/\zeta(s)\) decreases from infinity to one on
228-\((2,\infty)\), while
229-\(\rho_3=\zeta(2)/\zeta(3)<2\).  The defining equation
230-\(\rho_{\sigma_0}=2\) therefore puts \(2<\sigma_0<3\), hence
231-\(1<\alpha<2\).  Finally, \Cref{lem:critical-tail-summation} gives
232-\(\overline F_C(j)\sim K_Cj^{-\alpha}\).  All three hypotheses of
233:\Cref{lem:second-order-lattice-renewal} now hold.  Its conclusion is
234-\[
235- u_j(\sigma_0)=\frac1{\mu_C}
236- +\frac{K_C}{\mu_C^2(\sigma_0-2)}j^{2-\sigma_0}
237- +o(j^{2-\sigma_0}),
238-\]
239-as claimed.
240-\end{proof}

242-\begin{lemma}[One-sided stable domain of attraction]
243-\label{lem:one-sided-stable-domain}
244-Let \(X\ge0\) have finite mean \(\mu\) and
245-\(\Pr\{X>x\}\sim Kx^{-\alpha}\), where \(K>0\) and
--
292-\[
293- n\EE\left[e^{itX/a_n}-1-\frac{itX}{a_n}\right]
294- \longrightarrow
295- \int_0^\infty(e^{itx}-1-itx)\alpha x^{-\alpha-1}\,dx.
296-\]
297-Finally, \(n/a_n^2\to0\) because \(\alpha<2\).  Expanding the logarithm
298-of the characteristic function therefore shows that centering by the full
299-mean \(n\mu\) produces exactly the displayed exponent.  This proves
300-\eqref{eq:generic-one-sided-stable-limit} and also shows why no negative
301-tail term or unspecified scale factor remains.
302-\end{proof}
303-
304:\begin{proof}[Proof of \Cref{thm:arithmetic-critical-stable-renewal}]
305:Equation \eqref{eq:critical-renewal-normalization} makes the displayed
306-letter weights a probability measure.  The local estimate and both tail
307-constants follow from \Cref{lem:critical-tail-summation}.
308:\Cref{prop:critical-renewal-hypotheses} gives
309:\eqref{eq:critical-renewal-second-order} with every lattice-renewal
310-hypothesis verified.
311-
312:Because \(2-\sigma_0\in(-1,0)\), summation of the renewal remainder is
313-legitimate: if \(r_j=o(j^{2-\sigma_0})\), then for every
314-\(\varepsilon>0\),
315-\[
316- \left|\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}r_j\right|
317- \le O_\varepsilon(1)+\varepsilon
318-      \sum_{j=1}^{m-1}j^{2-\sigma_0}
319- =o(m^{3-\sigma_0}).
320-\]
321-Also,
322-\[
323- \sum_{j=1}^{m-1}j^{2-\sigma_0}
324- \sim\frac{m^{3-\sigma_0}}{3-\sigma_0}.
325-\]
326-Substitution in the exact identity
327:\eqref{eq:critical-weighted-renewal-identity} gives
328-\eqref{eq:standard-layer-critical-second-order}.  The terminal term
329-\(u_m=O(1)\) and the endpoint \(-1\) are
330-\(o(m^{3-\sigma_0})\).
331-
332-Finally, take \(X=C\) in \Cref{lem:one-sided-stable-domain}.  The finite
333:mean was verified in \Cref{prop:critical-renewal-hypotheses}, and
334-\Cref{lem:critical-tail-summation} supplies its tail with \(K=K_C\).
335-This proves \eqref{eq:critical-cost-stable-domain} and
336-\eqref{eq:centered-stable-characteristic-exponent}.  It also gives the
337-explicit normalization \eqref{eq:explicit-critical-stable-normalization}.
338-\end{proof}

340-\begin{corollary}[Critical asymptotic for the classical Fibonacci partition function]
341-\label{cor:classical-fibonacci-critical-asymptotic}
342-Let \(R(N)\) count the representations of \(N\) as a sum of distinct
343-members of \(F_2,F_3,\ldots\), as in
344-\eqref{eq:fibonacci-partition-function}.  Then
345-\begin{align}
346- &\sum_{F_{m+1}-1\le N<F_{m+2}-1}R(N)^{-\sigma_0}
347- \notag\\
348- &\qquad=\frac{2m}{\mu_C}
349- +\frac{2K_C}{\mu_C^2(\sigma_0-2)(3-\sigma_0)}
350-  m^{3-\sigma_0}+o(m^{3-\sigma_0}).
351- \label{eq:classical-fibonacci-critical-asymptotic}
352-\end{align}
353:Equivalently, relative to the known linear renewal scale,
354-\begin{equation}
355- \sum_{N\in\mathcal I_m}R(N)^{-\sigma_0}
356- =\frac{2m}{\mu_C}\left[
357- 1+\frac{K_C}{\mu_C(\sigma_0-2)(3-\sigma_0)}
358- m^{2-\sigma_0}+o(m^{2-\sigma_0})\right].
359- \label{eq:relative-fibonacci-critical-correction}
360-\end{equation}
361-The exponent \(3-\sigma_0\) lies strictly between zero and one.  Hence the
362-second term diverges on the absolute partition-function scale, is smaller
363-than the leading linear term, and cannot be absorbed into a bounded endpoint
364-correction.
365-\end{corollary}
366-
367-\begin{proof}
368-The interval in \eqref{eq:classical-fibonacci-critical-asymptotic} is
369-exactly \(\mathcal I_m\), so the left side is
370-\(Z_m^R(-\sigma_0)\).  Equation
371-\eqref{eq:standard-layer-critical-second-order} proves the first expansion.
372-Factoring out \(2m/\mu_C\) gives
373-\eqref{eq:relative-fibonacci-critical-correction}.  The inequalities
374-\(2<\sigma_0<3\), already verified in
375:\Cref{prop:critical-renewal-hypotheses}, give the stated scale comparison.
376-\end{proof}

sec_conclusion.tex-2-
sec_conclusion.tex-3-The denominator-weighted Brocot layer exhibits a complete one-digit
sec_conclusion.tex-4-condensation picture for every fixed \(s>2\).  Its nonmultiplicative
sec_conclusion.tex-5-continuant weight does not place it under a general independent-sum or Gibbs
sec_conclusion.tex:6:partition theorem.  The arithmetic replacement is the two-sided context
sec_conclusion.tex-7-estimate: after the large digit is removed, the inverse-continuant masses of
sec_conclusion.tex-8-the left and right words are summable and the central continuant is
sec_conclusion.tex-9-asymptotically their product times the large digit.  This yields the product

sec_introduction.tex-208-particular, total-variation one-big-jump results and Gibbs-partition
sec_introduction.tex-209-condensation provide useful comparisons

sec_introduction.tex-211-here.  A continuant weight does not factor into weights of its digits, so the
sec_introduction.tex-212-model is not an independent allocation or a multiplicative Gibbs partition.
sec_introduction.tex:213:The proof instead exploits the exact two-sided continuant inequality and the
sec_introduction.tex-214-summability of all finite contexts.  Accordingly, the result is presented as
sec_introduction.tex-215-a structural theorem for this nonmultiplicative arithmetic family, not as a
sec_introduction.tex-216-general condensation principle.

 succeeded in 268ms:
 130: This is \eqref{eq:tail-summation-expanded}, and the last constant is
 131: \(K_C=2^{\sigma_0+2}/(\sigma_0-1)\).
 132: \end{proof}
 133: 
 134: \begin{lemma}[Second-order lattice renewal input]
 135: \label{lem:second-order-lattice-renewal}
 136: Let \((p_j)_{j\ge1}\) be a probability mass function on the positive
 137: integers.  Suppose that
 138: \begin{enumerate}
 139:  \item \(\gcd\{j:p_j>0\}=1\), so the renewal law is aperiodic of span one;
 140:  \item \(\mu=\sum_{j\ge1}jp_j<\infty\);
 141:  \item its tail \(\overline F(j)=\sum_{k>j}p_k\) satisfies
 142:  \(\overline F(j)\sim Kj^{-\alpha}\), where \(K>0\) and
 143:  \(\alpha>1\).
 144: \end{enumerate}
 145: Define \(v_0=1\) and
 146: \(v_j=\sum_{k=1}^jp_kv_{j-k}\).  Then
 147: \begin{equation}
 148:  v_j=\frac1\mu+\frac{K}{\mu^2(\alpha-1)}j^{1-\alpha}
 149:       +o(j^{1-\alpha}).
 150:  \label{eq:second-order-renewal-input}
 151: \end{equation}
 152: \end{lemma}
 153: 
 154: \begin{proof}
 155: Introduce the equilibrium mass and its tail
 156: \[
 157:  p^{\rm e}_j=\frac{\overline F(j-1)}\mu,
 158:  \qquad
 159:  \overline F^{\rm e}(j)
 160:  =\sum_{k>j}p^{\rm e}_k
 161:  =\frac1\mu\sum_{r>j}\overline F(r).
 162: \]
 163: The second-order assertion used here is the first displayed renewal
 164: asymptotic in Omey and Van Gulck
 165: \cite[Sec.~3.2, first assertion following Eq.~(8)]{OmeyVanGulck2015}:
 166: under the three hypotheses above,
 167: \begin{equation}
 168:  v_j-\frac1\mu\sim\frac{\overline F^{\rm e}(j)}\mu.
 169:  \label{eq:omey-vangulck-equilibrium-form}
 170: \end{equation}
 171: Their paper states the result for a tail regularly varying with index
 172: \(-\alpha\), \(\alpha>1\); their \(u_n\), \(\mu\), and
 173: \(\overline F_n\) are respectively \(v_j\), \(\mu\), and
 174: \(\overline F(j)\) here.  Discrete Karamata summation supplies the constant:
 175: \[
 176:  \overline F^{\rm e}(j)
 177:  =\frac1\mu\sum_{r>j}\overline F(r)
 178:  \sim\frac{K}{\mu(\alpha-1)}j^{1-\alpha}.
 179: \]
 180: Substitution in \eqref{eq:omey-vangulck-equilibrium-form} gives
 181: \eqref{eq:second-order-renewal-input}.  Thus neither the power nor its
 182: coefficient is hidden in the citation.
 183: \end{proof}
 184: 
 185: \begin{proposition}[Verification for the critical letter law]
 186: \label{prop:critical-renewal-hypotheses}
 187: The mass function \(p_j=b_j(\sigma_0)\) satisfies every hypothesis of
 188: \Cref{lem:second-order-lattice-renewal}, with
 189: \[
 190:  \mu=\mu_C,\qquad \alpha=\sigma_0-1\in(1,2),\qquad K=K_C.
 191: \]
 192: Consequently \eqref{eq:critical-renewal-second-order} holds.
 193: \end{proposition}
 194: 
 195: \begin{proof}
 196: First, \eqref{eq:weighted-renewal-at-one} and
 197: \eqref{eq:critical-renewal-normalization} give
 198: \[
 199:  \sum_{j\ge1}p_j=B_{\sigma_0}(1)=1,
 200: \]
 201: so this is a probability mass function.  Every cost is a positive odd
 202: integer.  More particularly, the negative continued fractions
 203: \(1/2=[0;2]_-\) and \(1/3=[0;3]_-\) have
 204: \[
 205:  c(1/2)=2(2-1)+1=3,\qquad
 206:  c(1/3)=2(3-1)+1=5,
 207: \]
 208: and their masses are \(2^{-\sigma_0}>0\) and
 209: \(3^{-\sigma_0}>0\).  Hence
 210: \[
 211:  \gcd\{j:p_j>0\}\mid\gcd(3,5)=1.
 212: \]
 213: The renewal span is therefore one.  Equivalently, sums of threes and fives
 214: contain every integer from eight onward, so the renewal epochs have no
 215: nontrivial period.  The fact that each individual increment is odd does not
 216: create a period, because the number of increments is not fixed.
 217: 
 218: Second, the mean is exactly the series in
 219: \eqref{eq:critical-cost-mean}.  The Panov--Liehl estimate recorded after
 220: that equation gives
 221: \[
 222:  \mu_C\ll\sum_{q\ge2}q^{1-\sigma_0}(\log q)^2<\infty
 223: \]
 224: because \(\sigma_0>2\).  Thus the mean hypothesis is verified directly
 225: against the letter law, not inferred from its tail alone.
 226: 
 227: Third, \(\rho_s=\zeta(s-1)/\zeta(s)\) decreases from infinity to one on
 228: \((2,\infty)\), while
 229: \(\rho_3=\zeta(2)/\zeta(3)<2\).  The defining equation
 230: \(\rho_{\sigma_0}=2\) therefore puts \(2<\sigma_0<3\), hence
 231: \(1<\alpha<2\).  Finally, \Cref{lem:critical-tail-summation} gives
 232: \(\overline F_C(j)\sim K_Cj^{-\alpha}\).  All three hypotheses of
 233: \Cref{lem:second-order-lattice-renewal} now hold.  Its conclusion is
 234: \[
 235:  u_j(\sigma_0)=\frac1{\mu_C}
 236:  +\frac{K_C}{\mu_C^2(\sigma_0-2)}j^{2-\sigma_0}
 237:  +o(j^{2-\sigma_0}),
 238: \]
 239: as claimed.
 240: \end{proof}
 241: 
 242: \begin{lemma}[One-sided stable domain of attraction]
 243: \label{lem:one-sided-stable-domain}
 244: Let \(X\ge0\) have finite mean \(\mu\) and
 245: \(\Pr\{X>x\}\sim Kx^{-\alpha}\), where \(K>0\) and
 246: \(1<\alpha<2\).  Put \(a_n=(Kn)^{1/\alpha}\).  Then
 247: \begin{equation}
 248:  \frac{X_1+\cdots+X_n-n\mu}{a_n}
 249:  \xrightarrow{\rm d}S_\alpha,
 250:  \label{eq:generic-one-sided-stable-limit}
 251: \end{equation}
 252: where
 253: \[
 254:  \log\EE e^{itS_\alpha}
 255:  =\int_0^\infty(e^{itx}-1-itx)\alpha x^{-\alpha-1}\,dx.
 256: \]
 257: The limiting L\'evy measure has density
 258: \(\alpha x^{-\alpha-1}\) on \((0,\infty)\) and zero mass on
 259: \(( -\infty,0)\).
 260: \end{lemma}
 261: 
 262: \begin{proof}
 263: The one-sided criterion is Feller's stable domain-of-attraction theorem
 264: \cite[Ch.~XVII, Sec.~5, Thm.~2]{Feller1971}.  We verify its tail balance
 265: and normalization explicitly.  Since \(a_n^\alpha=Kn\), for every \(x>0\),
 266: \begin{equation}
 267:  n\Pr\{X>a_nx\}\longrightarrow x^{-\alpha},
 268:  \qquad
 269:  n\Pr\{X<-a_nx\}=0.
 270:  \label{eq:stable-tail-balance}
 271: \end{equation}
 272: Thus the positive and negative tail-balance constants are one and zero.
 273: In particular \(n\Pr\{X>a_n\}\to1\).
 274: 
 275: For completeness, the characteristic exponent follows directly from these
 276: conditions.  Let \(\nu_n=n\Pr\{X/a_n\in\cdot\}\).  Equation
 277: \eqref{eq:stable-tail-balance} gives vague convergence on
 278: \((0,\infty)\) to the measure
 279: \(\nu(dx)=\alpha x^{-\alpha-1}dx\).  The parts near zero and infinity can
 280: be removed uniformly.  Indeed, integration by parts and regular variation
 281: give
 282: \begin{align*}
 283:  \frac n{a_n^2}\EE[X^2\mathbf1_{\{X\le\varepsilon a_n\}}]
 284:  &\longrightarrow\frac{\alpha}{2-\alpha}\varepsilon^{2-\alpha},\\
 285:  \frac n{a_n}\EE[X\mathbf1_{\{X>Ma_n\}}]
 286:  &\longrightarrow\frac{\alpha}{\alpha-1}M^{1-\alpha}.
 287: \end{align*}
 288: The first bound tends to zero with \(\varepsilon\downarrow0\), and the
 289: second with \(M\to\infty\).  Since
 290: \(e^{itx}-1-itx=O(x^2)\) near zero and is \(O(1+x)\) at infinity, vague
 291: convergence extends to this compensated test function:
 292: \[
 293:  n\EE\left[e^{itX/a_n}-1-\frac{itX}{a_n}\right]
 294:  \longrightarrow
 295:  \int_0^\infty(e^{itx}-1-itx)\alpha x^{-\alpha-1}\,dx.
 296: \]
 297: Finally, \(n/a_n^2\to0\) because \(\alpha<2\).  Expanding the logarithm
 298: of the characteristic function therefore shows that centering by the full
 299: mean \(n\mu\) produces exactly the displayed exponent.  This proves
 300: \eqref{eq:generic-one-sided-stable-limit} and also shows why no negative
 301: tail term or unspecified scale factor remains.
 302: \end{proof}
 303: 
 304: \begin{proof}[Proof of \Cref{thm:arithmetic-critical-stable-renewal}]
 305: Equation \eqref{eq:critical-renewal-normalization} makes the displayed
 306: letter weights a probability measure.  The local estimate and both tail
 307: constants follow from \Cref{lem:critical-tail-summation}.
 308: \Cref{prop:critical-renewal-hypotheses} gives
 309: \eqref{eq:critical-renewal-second-order} with every lattice-renewal
 310: hypothesis verified.
 311: 
 312: Because \(2-\sigma_0\in(-1,0)\), summation of the renewal remainder is
 313: legitimate: if \(r_j=o(j^{2-\sigma_0})\), then for every
 314: \(\varepsilon>0\),
 315: \[
 316:  \left|\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}r_j\right|
 317:  \le O_\varepsilon(1)+\varepsilon
 318:       \sum_{j=1}^{m-1}j^{2-\sigma_0}
 319:  =o(m^{3-\sigma_0}).
 320: \]
 321: Also,
 322: \[
 323:  \sum_{j=1}^{m-1}j^{2-\sigma_0}
 324:  \sim\frac{m^{3-\sigma_0}}{3-\sigma_0}.
 325: \]
 326: Substitution in the exact identity
 327: \eqref{eq:critical-weighted-renewal-identity} gives
 328: \eqref{eq:standard-layer-critical-second-order}.  The terminal term
 329: \(u_m=O(1)\) and the endpoint \(-1\) are
 330: \(o(m^{3-\sigma_0})\).
 331: 
 332: Finally, take \(X=C\) in \Cref{lem:one-sided-stable-domain}.  The finite
 333: mean was verified in \Cref{prop:critical-renewal-hypotheses}, and
 334: \Cref{lem:critical-tail-summation} supplies its tail with \(K=K_C\).
 335: This proves \eqref{eq:critical-cost-stable-domain} and
 336: \eqref{eq:centered-stable-characteristic-exponent}.  It also gives the
 337: explicit normalization \eqref{eq:explicit-critical-stable-normalization}.
 338: \end{proof}
 339: 
 340: \begin{corollary}[Critical asymptotic for the classical Fibonacci partition function]
 341: \label{cor:classical-fibonacci-critical-asymptotic}
 342: Let \(R(N)\) count the representations of \(N\) as a sum of distinct
 343: members of \(F_2,F_3,\ldots\), as in
 344: \eqref{eq:fibonacci-partition-function}.  Then
 345: \begin{align}
 346:  &\sum_{F_{m+1}-1\le N<F_{m+2}-1}R(N)^{-\sigma_0}
 347:  \notag\\
 348:  &\qquad=\frac{2m}{\mu_C}
 349:  +\frac{2K_C}{\mu_C^2(\sigma_0-2)(3-\sigma_0)}
 350:   m^{3-\sigma_0}+o(m^{3-\sigma_0}).
 351:  \label{eq:classical-fibonacci-critical-asymptotic}
 352: \end{align}
 353: Equivalently, relative to the known linear renewal scale,
 354: \begin{equation}
 355:  \sum_{N\in\mathcal I_m}R(N)^{-\sigma_0}
 356:  =\frac{2m}{\mu_C}\left[
 357:  1+\frac{K_C}{\mu_C(\sigma_0-2)(3-\sigma_0)}
 358:  m^{2-\sigma_0}+o(m^{2-\sigma_0})\right].
 359:  \label{eq:relative-fibonacci-critical-correction}
 360: \end{equation}
 361: The exponent \(3-\sigma_0\) lies strictly between zero and one.  Hence the
 362: second term diverges on the absolute partition-function scale, is smaller
 363: than the leading linear term, and cannot be absorbed into a bounded endpoint
 364: correction.
 365: \end{corollary}
 366: 
 367: \begin{proof}
 368: The interval in \eqref{eq:classical-fibonacci-critical-asymptotic} is
 369: exactly \(\mathcal I_m\), so the left side is
 370: \(Z_m^R(-\sigma_0)\).  Equation
 371: \eqref{eq:standard-layer-critical-second-order} proves the first expansion.
 372: Factoring out \(2m/\mu_C\) gives
 373: \eqref{eq:relative-fibonacci-critical-correction}.  The inequalities
 374: \(2<\sigma_0<3\), already verified in
 375: \Cref{prop:critical-renewal-hypotheses}, give the stated scale comparison.
 376: \end{proof}

 379: \label{rem:critical-constant-numerics}
 380: Solving the defining zeta equation gives

   1: \section{Conclusion}\label{sec:conclusion}
   2: 
   3: The denominator-weighted Brocot layer exhibits a complete one-digit
   4: condensation picture for every fixed \(s>2\).  Its nonmultiplicative
   5: continuant weight does not place it under a general independent-sum or Gibbs
   6: partition theorem.  The arithmetic replacement is the two-sided context
   7: estimate: after the large digit is removed, the inverse-continuant masses of
   8: the left and right words are summable and the central continuant is
   9: asymptotically their product times the large digit.  This yields the product
  10: context law in total variation and all of its defect, location, and
  11: denominator consequences.  In particular, the result concerns the entire
  12: sampled continued fraction rather than only its normalizing sum: uniqueness
  13: of the large digit, tightness of the discarded digit mass, and convergence
  14: of both contexts are simultaneous conclusions.
  15: 
  16: The same proof has a scalar output.  It gives the coefficient
  17: \(2\{\zeta(s-1)/\zeta(s)\}^2\), identifies the endpoint overcount in the
  18: earlier convolution, and transfers termwise to the odd denominator layers
  19: used by Fibonacci generators.  The correction concerns only that leading
  20: coefficient.  A determination of the remaining coefficients would require
  21: uniform expansions beyond the dominated-convergence argument used here.
  22: Nothing in the endpoint calculation verifies, modifies, or rules out those
  23: remaining coefficients.  This boundary is essential because the
  24: condensation proof uses a first-order central-continuant approximation and
  25: summable domination; it does not produce the uniform higher-order remainder
  26: needed for a full corrected expansion.
  27: 
  28: At the arithmetic critical exponent, the scalar output becomes an exact
  29: regularly varying generator-cost law.  The free-generator orbit count then
  30: turns the classical Fibonacci layer sum into a renewal sum.  The tail
  31: constant \(b_C=8\) controls both the spectrally positive stable limit and the
  32: fractional \(m^{3-\sigma_0}\) term.  This is the full shared mechanism:
  33: continued-fraction contexts determine the critical tail, and renewal theory
  34: transfers that tail to the Fibonacci partition function.
  35: 
  36: The transfer has two distinct probabilistic outputs.  For independent
  37: critical generator letters, the explicit scale
  38: \[
  39:  a_n=\left(\frac{2^{\sigma_0+2}}{\sigma_0-1}n\right)^{1/(\sigma_0-1)}
  40: \]
  41: gives a centered spectrally positive stable limit.  For the deterministic
  42: classical partition function, the exact orbit identity converts the same
  43: tail into
  44: \begin{align*}
  45:  &\sum_{F_{m+1}-1\le N<F_{m+2}-1}R(N)^{-\sigma_0}\\
  46:  &\qquad=\frac{2m}{\mu_C}
  47:  +\frac{2K_C}{\mu_C^2(\sigma_0-2)(3-\sigma_0)}m^{3-\sigma_0}
  48:  +o(m^{3-\sigma_0}).
  49: \end{align*}
  50: The second term is unbounded but sublinear.  It is therefore a genuine
  51: finite-size contribution at the scale of the partition sum, rather than a
  52: relabeling of the finitely many endpoint orbits.
  53: 
  54: Several limitations follow from the proof.  The stable statement is a
  55: domain-of-attraction result for sums of generator-letter costs; it is not a
  56: local limit theorem and supplies no rate of convergence.  The Fibonacci
  57: expansion is established at the single arithmetic critical exponent and
  58: does not assert an analogous fractional correction away from criticality.
  59: Nor does the argument classify other continued-fraction weights: the exact
  60: constant uses the regular/negative continued-fraction correspondence, the
  61: two context masses, and Weinstein's particular free monoid.  Models without
  62: those identities require separate arithmetic input.
  63: 
  64: The companion manuscript \emph{Finite-window Zeckendorf thermodynamics}
  65: starts from an exact finite-window correspondence and studies local spectra,
  66: critical coexistence, and large deviations.  Those questions are logically
  67: independent of the total-variation condensation theorem, and the two
  68: arguments are logically autonomous: no result of the companion manuscript
  69: is used here, while its principal finite-window results do not require the
  70: tail constant, stable law, or fractional correction proved here.  Keeping
  71: the two settings separate also makes the scope of the present conclusion
  72: explicit.  The argument establishes structural consequences specific to
  73: this nonmultiplicative continuant model and its Fibonacci generator renewal;
  74: it does not establish a new universal mechanism for condensation or
  75: freezing.

sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-143-\begin{corollary}[Layer generating function and adjacent layers]
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-144-\label{cor:fibonacci-layer-generating-function}
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-145-As a formal power series in \(z\),
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-146-\begin{equation}
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex:147: \sum_{m\ge1}Z_m^R(-s)z^m
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-148- =\frac{(1+z)U_s(z)-1}{1-z}
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex:149: =\frac{1+z}{(1-z)(1-B_s(z))}-\frac1{1-z}.
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-150- \label{eq:fibonacci-layer-generating-function}
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-151-\end{equation}
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-152-Equivalently, the change between two adjacent standard Fibonacci layers is
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-153-\begin{equation}
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex:154: Z_{m+1}^R(-s)-Z_m^R(-s)=u_m(s)+u_{m+1}(s),
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-155- \qquad m\ge1.
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-156- \label{eq:adjacent-fibonacci-layer-increment}
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-157-\end{equation}
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-158-\end{corollary}
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-159-
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-160-\begin{proof}
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-161-Multiply the second identity in \eqref{eq:standard-layer-renewal} by \(z^m\)
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-162-and sum over \(m\ge1\).  Formal rearrangement gives
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-163-\begin{align*}
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex:164: \sum_{m\ge1}Z_m^R(-s)z^m
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-165- &=\frac{2z}{1-z}U_s(z)+U_s(z)-1-\frac z{1-z}\\
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-166- &=\frac{(1+z)U_s(z)-1}{1-z}.
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-167-\end{align*}
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-168-Inserting \eqref{eq:weighted-renewal-generating-function} proves the second
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-169-form in \eqref{eq:fibonacci-layer-generating-function}.  Subtracting
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-170-\eqref{eq:standard-layer-renewal} at \(m\) from the same identity at
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-171-\(m+1\) gives \eqref{eq:adjacent-fibonacci-layer-increment}.  Thus the
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-172-standard layer sum, its discrete layer derivative, and the renewal
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-173-generating function are exact transforms of one another.
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-174-\end{proof}

sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-176-\begin{lemma}[Letter words and renewal coefficients]

sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-183-\end{equation}
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-184-For fixed \(j\) the outer sum is finite.  If \(B_s(1)=1\), so that
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-185-\(b_\ell(s)\) is a probability mass function, and if

sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-188-\begin{equation}

sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-190- \label{eq:probabilistic-renewal-mass}
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-191-\end{equation}
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-192-Here the term \(r=0\) is \(1\) for \(j=0\) and \(0\) otherwise.
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-193-\end{lemma}
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-194-
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-195-\begin{proof}
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex:196:The coefficient of \(z^j\) in \(B_s(z)^r\) is
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-197-\[
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-198- \sum_{\substack{\ell_1+\cdots+\ell_r=j\\\ell_i\ge1}}

sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-201-Expanding each letter sum in

sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-203-in \eqref{eq:renewal-coefficient-word-sum}.  Every letter has cost at least
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-204-three, so only \(r\le j/3\) can occur.  Summing over \(r\) and using the
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-205-formal geometric series
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex:206:\(\sum_{r\ge0}B_s(z)^r=(1-B_s(z))^{-1}\) proves
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-207-\eqref{eq:renewal-coefficient-word-sum}.  Under the normalization
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-208-\(B_s(1)=1\), the same Cauchy product is the convolution probability for
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-209-the sum of \(r\) independent costs, which gives
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-210-\eqref{eq:probabilistic-renewal-mass}.
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-211-\end{proof}
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-212-
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-213-The critical normalization follows directly from the totient Dirichlet
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-214-series.  For \(s>2\), every denominator \(q\ge2\) occurs with its
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-215-\(\varphi(q)\) reduced numerators, so Tonelli's theorem gives
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-216-\begin{equation}
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-217- B_s(1)=\sum_{q\ge2}\frac{\varphi(q)}{q^s}
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-218- =\frac{\zeta(s-1)}{\zeta(s)}-1.
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex:219: \label{eq:weighted-renewal-at-one}
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-220-\end{equation}
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-221-The right side decreases continuously from infinity to zero as \(s\) runs
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-222-from \(2\) to infinity.  Thus there is a unique \(\sigma_0>2\) with
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-223-\begin{equation}
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-224- \frac{\zeta(\sigma_0-1)}{\zeta(\sigma_0)}=2,
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-225- \qquad B_{\sigma_0}(1)=1.
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-226- \label{eq:critical-renewal-normalization}
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-227-\end{equation}

sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-246-argument below.
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-247-
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-248-Combining the normalization with
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-249-\Cref{lem:letter-word-renewal-coefficients,prop:weighted-generator-renewal}
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-250-puts the identity needed in the next section in purely probabilistic form.
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-251-If \(S_0=0\) and \(S_r=C_1+\cdots+C_r\), then
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-252-\begin{equation}
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-253-\begin{aligned}
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex:254: u_j(\sigma_0)&=\sum_{r\ge0}\Pr\{S_r=j\},\\
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex:255: Z_m^R(-\sigma_0)
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex:256: &=2\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}u_j(\sigma_0)+u_m(\sigma_0)-1\\
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-257- &=2\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}\sum_{r\ge0}\Pr\{S_r=j\}
sec_fibonacci_renewal.tex-258-  +\sum_{r\ge0}\Pr\{S_r=m\}-1.

   1: \section{The arithmetic critical law}\label{sec:arithmetic-critical}
   2: 
   3: \begin{theorem}[Arithmetic critical stable renewal]
   4: \label{thm:arithmetic-critical-stable-renewal}
   5: Put \(\alpha=\sigma_0-1\in(1,2)\), and under the critical letter law put
   6: \[
   7:  \Pr\{(C,H)=(c(p/q),\log q)\}=q^{-\sigma_0}
   8:  \qquad(p/q\in\mathcal A).
   9: \]
  10: Then \(\Pr\{C=2d+1\}=b_{2d+1}(\sigma_0)\), and the constants
  11: \begin{equation}
  12:  \begin{aligned}
  13:  b_C&:=
  14:  \sum_{\mathbf u\in\mathcal W_{\rm L}}
  15:  \sum_{\mathbf v\in\mathcal W_{\rm R}}
  16:  \{\mathcal K(\mathbf u)\mathcal K(\mathbf v)\}^{-\sigma_0}
  17:  =2\left(\frac{\zeta(\sigma_0-1)}{\zeta(\sigma_0)}\right)^2=8,\\
  18:  K_C&:=\frac{2^{\sigma_0-1}}{\sigma_0-1}b_C
  19:  =\frac{2^{\sigma_0+2}}{\sigma_0-1}
  20:  \end{aligned}
  21:  \label{eq:critical-tail-constants}
  22: \end{equation}
  23: are positive.  The double series defining \(b_C\) is absolutely convergent,
  24: and
  25: \begin{align}
  26:  b_{2d+1}(\sigma_0)&\sim b_Cd^{-\sigma_0},
  27:  &&d\to\infty,
  28:  \label{eq:critical-letter-local-tail}\\
  29:  \Pr\{C>x\}
  30:  &=\sum_{q\ge2}q^{-\sigma_0}
  31:    \card{\{1\le p<q:(p,q)=1,\ c(p/q)>x\}}\notag\\
  32:  &\sim K_Cx^{1-\sigma_0},
  33:  &&x\to\infty.
  34:  \label{eq:critical-letter-tail}
  35: \end{align}
  36: If \(u_j=u_j(\sigma_0)\) is the renewal sequence in
  37: \eqref{eq:weighted-renewal-coefficients}, then
  38: \begin{equation}
  39:  u_j=\frac1{\mu_C}
  40:  +\frac{K_C}{\mu_C^2(\sigma_0-2)}j^{2-\sigma_0}
  41:  +o(j^{2-\sigma_0}).
  42:  \label{eq:critical-renewal-second-order}
  43: \end{equation}
  44: Consequently,
  45: \begin{equation}
  46:  Z_m^R(-\sigma_0)
  47:  =\frac{2m}{\mu_C}
  48:  +\frac{2K_C}{\mu_C^2(\sigma_0-2)(3-\sigma_0)}
  49:    m^{3-\sigma_0}+o(m^{3-\sigma_0}).
  50:  \label{eq:standard-layer-critical-second-order}
  51: \end{equation}
  52: Finally, \(C\) belongs to the domain of attraction of a spectrally positive
  53: \(\alpha\)-stable law.  More precisely, if \(a_n>0\) is chosen so that
  54: \(n\Pr\{C>a_n\}\to1\), then for independent copies \(C_1,C_2,\ldots\),
  55: \begin{equation}
  56:  \frac{C_1+\cdots+C_n-n\mu_C}{a_n}
  57:  \xrightarrow{\mathrm d}\mathcal S_\alpha,
  58:  \label{eq:critical-cost-stable-domain}
  59: \end{equation}
  60: where the centered spectrally positive law \(\mathcal S_\alpha\) is fixed by
  61: \begin{equation}
  62:  \log\EE e^{it\mathcal S_\alpha}
  63:  =\int_0^\infty
  64:    (e^{itx}-1-itx)\alpha x^{-\alpha-1}\,dx.
  65:  \label{eq:centered-stable-characteristic-exponent}
  66: \end{equation}
  67: In particular, the explicit choice
  68: \begin{equation}
  69:  a_n=(K_Cn)^{1/\alpha}
  70:  =\left(\frac{2^{\sigma_0+2}}{\sigma_0-1}n\right)^{1/(\sigma_0-1)}
  71:  \label{eq:explicit-critical-stable-normalization}
  72: \end{equation}
  73: satisfies the required tail normalization.
  74: \end{theorem}
  75: 
  76: The proof is separated into the arithmetic tail calculation, the lattice
  77: renewal input, and the one-sided stable criterion.  This also fixes the
  78: normalizations at each transfer.
  79: 
  80: \begin{lemma}[From the local denominator layer to the cost tail]
  81: \label{lem:critical-tail-summation}
  82: At \(s=\sigma_0\), the context series defining \(b_C\) is absolutely
  83: convergent and
  84: \[
  85:  b_C=2\left(\frac{\zeta(\sigma_0-1)}{\zeta(\sigma_0)}\right)^2=8.
  86: \]
  87: If \(\alpha=\sigma_0-1\), then
  88: \begin{equation}
  89:  \overline F_C(x):=\Pr\{C>x\}
  90:  \sim \frac{2^\alpha b_C}{\alpha}x^{-\alpha}
  91:  =K_Cx^{-\alpha}.
  92:  \label{eq:tail-summation-expanded}
  93: \end{equation}
  94: In particular, \(\overline F_C\) is regularly varying with index
  95: \(-\alpha\).
  96: \end{lemma}
  97: 
  98: \begin{proof}
  99: The two context masses in \eqref{eq:brocot-context-masses} are finite for
 100: every \(s>2\), so their product is the absolutely convergent double series
 101: in \eqref{eq:critical-tail-constants}.  Their product is \(2\rho_s^2\).
 102: At the critical point \(\rho_{\sigma_0}=2\), which proves \(b_C=8\).
 103: 
 104: By \Cref{thm:arithmetic-denominator-layer}, with \(d\) denoting the
 105: negative-continuant depth,
 106: \[
 107:  \Pr\{C=2d+1\}=b_{2d+1}(\sigma_0)
 108:  =b_Cd^{-\sigma_0}(1+o(1)).
 109: \]
 110: For real \(x\), the inequality \(2d+1>x\) is equivalent to
 111: \(d>(x-1)/2\).  The integral comparison for a power sequence, or the
 112: discrete Karamata theorem, gives
 113: \[
 114:  \sum_{d>y}d^{-\sigma_0}
 115:  \sim\int_y^\infty t^{-\sigma_0}\,dt
 116:  =\frac{y^{1-\sigma_0}}{\sigma_0-1}.
 117: \]
 118: The same conclusion holds after multiplication by a sequence tending to
 119: \(b_C\): for any \(\varepsilon>0\), all sufficiently large summands lie
 120: between \((b_C-\varepsilon)d^{-\sigma_0}\) and
 121: \((b_C+\varepsilon)d^{-\sigma_0}\), and the finitely many omitted terms do
 122: not enter a tail.  Therefore
 123: \begin{align*}
 124:  \overline F_C(x)
 125:  &=\sum_{d>(x-1)/2}b_{2d+1}(\sigma_0)\\
 126:  &\sim\frac{b_C}{\sigma_0-1}
 127:        \left(\frac{x-1}{2}\right)^{1-\sigma_0}
 128:  =\frac{2^\alpha b_C}{\alpha}x^{-\alpha}.
 129: \end{align*}
 130: This is \eqref{eq:tail-summation-expanded}, and the last constant is
 131: \(K_C=2^{\sigma_0+2}/(\sigma_0-1)\).
 132: \end{proof}
 304: \begin{proof}[Proof of \Cref{thm:arithmetic-critical-stable-renewal}]
 305: Equation \eqref{eq:critical-renewal-normalization} makes the displayed
 306: letter weights a probability measure.  The local estimate and both tail
 307: constants follow from \Cref{lem:critical-tail-summation}.
 308: \Cref{prop:critical-renewal-hypotheses} gives
 309: \eqref{eq:critical-renewal-second-order} with every lattice-renewal
 310: hypothesis verified.
 311: 
 312: Because \(2-\sigma_0\in(-1,0)\), summation of the renewal remainder is
 313: legitimate: if \(r_j=o(j^{2-\sigma_0})\), then for every
 314: \(\varepsilon>0\),
 315: \[
 316:  \left|\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}r_j\right|
 317:  \le O_\varepsilon(1)+\varepsilon
 318:       \sum_{j=1}^{m-1}j^{2-\sigma_0}
 319:  =o(m^{3-\sigma_0}).
 320: \]
 321: Also,
 322: \[
 323:  \sum_{j=1}^{m-1}j^{2-\sigma_0}
 324:  \sim\frac{m^{3-\sigma_0}}{3-\sigma_0}.
 325: \]
 326: Substitution in the exact identity
 327: \eqref{eq:critical-weighted-renewal-identity} gives
 328: \eqref{eq:standard-layer-critical-second-order}.  The terminal term
 329: \(u_m=O(1)\) and the endpoint \(-1\) are
 330: \(o(m^{3-\sigma_0})\).
 331: 
 332: Finally, take \(X=C\) in \Cref{lem:one-sided-stable-domain}.  The finite
 333: mean was verified in \Cref{prop:critical-renewal-hypotheses}, and
 334: \Cref{lem:critical-tail-summation} supplies its tail with \(K=K_C\).
 335: This proves \eqref{eq:critical-cost-stable-domain} and
 336: \eqref{eq:centered-stable-characteristic-exponent}.  It also gives the
 337: explicit normalization \eqref{eq:explicit-critical-stable-normalization}.
 338: \end{proof}
 339: 
 340: \begin{corollary}[Critical asymptotic for the classical Fibonacci partition function]
 341: \label{cor:classical-fibonacci-critical-asymptotic}
 342: Let \(R(N)\) count the representations of \(N\) as a sum of distinct
 343: members of \(F_2,F_3,\ldots\), as in
 344: \eqref{eq:fibonacci-partition-function}.  Then
 345: \begin{align}
 346:  &\sum_{F_{m+1}-1\le N<F_{m+2}-1}R(N)^{-\sigma_0}
 347:  \notag\\
 348:  &\qquad=\frac{2m}{\mu_C}
 349:  +\frac{2K_C}{\mu_C^2(\sigma_0-2)(3-\sigma_0)}
 350:   m^{3-\sigma_0}+o(m^{3-\sigma_0}).
 351:  \label{eq:classical-fibonacci-critical-asymptotic}
 352: \end{align}
 353: Equivalently, relative to the known linear renewal scale,
 354: \begin{equation}
 355:  \sum_{N\in\mathcal I_m}R(N)^{-\sigma_0}
 356:  =\frac{2m}{\mu_C}\left[
 357:  1+\frac{K_C}{\mu_C(\sigma_0-2)(3-\sigma_0)}
 358:  m^{2-\sigma_0}+o(m^{2-\sigma_0})\right].
 359:  \label{eq:relative-fibonacci-critical-correction}
 360: \end{equation}
 361: The exponent \(3-\sigma_0\) lies strictly between zero and one.  Hence the
 362: second term diverges on the absolute partition-function scale, is smaller
 363: than the leading linear term, and cannot be absorbed into a bounded endpoint
 364: correction.
 365: \end{corollary}
 366: 
 367: \begin{proof}
 368: The interval in \eqref{eq:classical-fibonacci-critical-asymptotic} is
 369: exactly \(\mathcal I_m\), so the left side is
 370: \(Z_m^R(-\sigma_0)\).  Equation
 371: \eqref{eq:standard-layer-critical-second-order} proves the first expansion.
 372: Factoring out \(2m/\mu_C\) gives
 373: \eqref{eq:relative-fibonacci-critical-correction}.  The inequalities
 374: \(2<\sigma_0<3\), already verified in
 375: \Cref{prop:critical-renewal-hypotheses}, give the stated scale comparison.
 376: \end{proof}
