 378: \begin{remark}[Numerical consistency check]
 379: \label{rem:critical-constant-numerics}
 380: Solving the defining zeta equation gives
 381: \begin{align*}
 382:  \sigma_0&=2.478750785733960\ldots,&
 383:  3-\sigma_0&=0.521249214266040\ldots,\\
 384:  K_C&=15.07798619025016\ldots.&&
 385: \end{align*}
 386: As an independent finite-sum check of the context constant, let
 387: \(\rho_Q=1+\sum_{q=2}^Q\varphi(q)q^{-\sigma_0}\).  The values of
 388: \(2\rho_Q^2\) for \(Q=10^3,10^4,10^5,10^6\) are respectively
 389: \begin{align*}
 390:  Q=10^3:\ &7.6323590814,& Q=10^4:\ &7.8769320348,\\
 391:  Q=10^5:\ &7.9590248673,& Q=10^6:\ &7.9863810907,
 392: \end{align*}
 393: consistent with the proved limit \(b_C=8\).  The computation is only a
 394: sanity check; the proof of the constant is the exact context evaluation in
 395: \Cref{lem:critical-tail-summation}.  The verification script and its unit
 396: test are included in the \texttt{artifacts} directory.
 397: \end{remark}

   1: \section{Introduction}\label{sec:introduction}
   2: 
   3: The Stern--Brocot and Farey constructions organize rational numbers by
   4: continued-fraction complexity.  A natural statistical question is what a
   5: typical rational looks like when a fixed complexity layer is biased toward
   6: small denominators.  In the model considered here, the complexity of the
   7: canonical regular continued fraction
   8: \[
   9:  x=[0;a_1,\ldots,a_r],\qquad a_i\ge1,\quad a_r\ge2,
  10: \]
  11: is the digit sum \(a_1+\cdots+a_r=n\), and the weight is
  12: \(\operatorname{den}(x)^{-s}\) for a fixed \(s>2\).  The continuant in the
  13: denominator is nonmultiplicative across the digits.  Nevertheless, a single
  14: partial quotient absorbs all but a tight amount of the digit sum, while the
  15: two finite contexts on its sides converge to independent arithmetic laws.
  16: 
  17: This condensation statement is stronger than the scalar asymptotic for the
  18: normalizing sum.  It identifies the limiting context words in total
  19: variation, the defect of the largest partial quotient, its position in the
  20: continued fraction, the numbers of digits on either side, and the leading
  21: factorization of the sampled denominator.  The proof also gives the scalar
  22: asymptotic with its exact constant.  That scalar consequence becomes the
  23: arithmetic input for a different but closely linked question: the critical
  24: renewal structure of the classical Fibonacci partition function.
  25: 
  26: The link is supplied by Weinstein's free generators for Fibonacci
  27: partitions \cite{Weinstein2016}.  Their letters are reduced fractions in
  28: \((0,1)\); a letter \(p/q\) carries multiplicative weight \(q\) and an odd
  29: cost read from its negative continued fraction.  The cost-\(2d+1\) letter
  30: sum is termwise identical to the regular-continuant sum at digit sum
  31: \(d+1\).  Thus the same scalar estimate that normalizes the condensation
  32: law determines the critical generator-cost tail.  At the unique
  33: \(\sigma_0>2\) satisfying
  34: \[
  35:  \frac{\zeta(\sigma_0-1)}{\zeta(\sigma_0)}=2,
  36: \]
  37: the generator letters form a probability distribution with a regularly
  38: varying tail of index \(1-\sigma_0\).  Its mean is finite and its variance is
  39: infinite.  A second-order lattice renewal theorem then produces a fractional
  40: power correction to the critical Fibonacci partition sum, and the same tail
  41: places the cost in the domain of attraction of a spectrally positive stable
  42: law.
  43: 
  44: \subsection{Main results}
  45: 
  46: For a finite word \(\mathbf w\), let \(\mathcal K(\mathbf w)\) be its
  47: regular continuant and let \(|\mathbf w|_1\) be its digit sum.  Write
  48: \(\mathcal W_{\rm L}\) for all finite positive words, including the empty
  49: word, and \(\mathcal W_{\rm R}\) for the canonical regular words, again
  50: including the empty word.  Put
  51: \[
  52:  \rho_s=\frac{\zeta(s-1)}{\zeta(s)}.
  53: \]
  54: The precise definitions occur in \Cref{sec:brocot-condensation}.  The first
  55: result is summarized as follows.
  56: 
  57: \begin{theoremA}[Total-variation condensation]
  58: For fixed \(s>2\), sample a canonical continued fraction of digit sum \(n\)
  59: with probability proportional to the inverse \(s\)-th power of its
  60: denominator.  With probability tending to one it has a unique digit larger
  61: than \(n/2\).  If \(U_n,V_n\) are the words to the left and right of that
  62: digit, then
  63: \[
  64:  (U_n,V_n)\xrightarrow{\rm TV}(U,V),
  65: \]
  66: where \(U,V\) are independent and
  67: \[
  68:  \Pr\{U=\mathbf u\}=\frac{\mathcal K(\mathbf u)^{-s}}{2\rho_s},
  69:  \qquad
  70:  \Pr\{V=\mathbf v\}=\frac{\mathcal K(\mathbf v)^{-s}}{\rho_s}.
  71: \]
  72: The defect, the location data, and the normalized denominator converge as
  73: stated in \Cref{thm:brocot-condensation}.
  74: \end{theoremA}
  75: 
  76: The asymmetry between the two context laws is caused only by the canonical
  77: terminal-digit convention.  A regular rational has two positive
  78: continued-fraction expansions, whereas the canonical form retains the one
  79: whose final digit is at least two.  The empty word and the one-letter word
  80: \((1)\) are endpoint exceptions.  Accounting for those exceptions is also
  81: what resolves the constant in the earlier scalar calculation.
  82: 
  83: Let
  84: \[
  85:  Z_n(s)=\sum_{\substack{a_1+\cdots+a_r=n\\a_i\ge1,\ a_r\ge2}}
  86:        \mathcal K(a_1,\ldots,a_r)^{-s}.
  87: \]
  88: Dushistova's notation is
  89: \(\sigma_\beta(n)=Z_n(2\beta)\).  The earlier paper obtains a fuller
  90: expansion whose displayed leading coefficient is
  91: \(R_s+2R_s^2\), where \(R_s=\rho_s\)
  92: \cite{Dushistova2007}.  The exact endpoint decomposition gives the following
  93: result.
  94: 
  95: \begin{theoremB}[Context constant and denominator layer]
  96: For every fixed \(s>2\),
  97: \[
  98:  Z_n(s)\sim2\rho_s^2n^{-s}.
  99: \]
 100: Under the termwise change from regular to negative continued fractions, the
 101: cost-\(2d+1\) denominator layer satisfies
 102: \[
 103:  b_{2d+1}(s)=Z_{d+1}(s)\sim2\rho_s^2d^{-s}.
 104: \]
 105: The constant \(2R_s^2\) corrects the leading coefficient in Dushistova's
 106: expansion.  No conclusion is drawn here about its other coefficients.
 107: \end{theoremB}
 108: 
 109: The proof is deliberately local.  The left context of digit sum zero
 110: contributes \(R_s\), the context of digit sum one contributes another
 111: \(R_s\), and the contexts of larger digit sum contribute
 112: \(2(R_s-1)R_s\).  Their sum is \(2R_s^2\).  In the printed convolution the
 113: condition excluding the empty left context is lost after the \(u=1\) term is
 114: separated; because the remaining convolution is doubled, that empty context
 115: is counted twice.  Restoring one subtraction removes precisely the excess
 116: \(R_s\).  This is not a change of continued-fraction convention or exponent.
 117: 
 118: For the probabilistic theorem, the central estimate is a summable
 119: two-context domination.  Once a digit \(a>n/2\) is singled out,
 120: multilinearity of the continuant gives
 121: \[
 122:  \mathcal K(\mathbf u,a,\mathbf v)
 123:  =a\mathcal K(\mathbf u)\mathcal K(\mathbf v)+O_{\mathbf u,\mathbf v}(1).
 124: \]
 125: The inverse-continuant mass of the two contexts is summable for \(s>2\).
 126: Words with no digit above \(n/2\) are negligible: a greedy block
 127: decomposition handles words made only of moderate digits, and a direct cut
 128: handles the cases with one or at least two larger digits.  Dominated
 129: convergence gives the product context law, and the discrete Scheff\'e
 130: argument upgrades pointwise convergence to total variation.
 131: 
 132: The second part of the paper transfers only the scalar layer estimate, not
 133: the total-variation theorem.  Let \(F_0=0,F_1=1\), and let \(R(N)\) count
 134: representations of \(N\) as a sum of distinct members of
 135: \(F_2,F_3,\ldots\).  On the standard Fibonacci layer
 136: \[
 137:  \mathcal I_m=[F_{m+1}-1,F_{m+2}-1),
 138: \]
 139: put
 140: \[
 141:  Z_m^R(t)=\sum_{N\in\mathcal I_m}R(N)^t.
 142: \]
 143: The free-generator parameterization yields a renewal sequence \(u_j(s)\)
 144: whose exact layer identity is
 145: \[
 146:  Z_m^R(-s)=2\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}u_j(s)+u_m(s)-1.
 147: \]
 148: This identity and its proof are given in
 149: \Cref{prop:weighted-generator-renewal}.
 150: 
 151: At \(s=\sigma_0\), let \(C\) be the generator-letter cost.  Set
 152: \(\alpha=\sigma_0-1\in(1,2)\), and let \(\mu_C=\EE C\).  The arithmetic
 153: constant from the two context masses is \(b_C=8\), while
 154: \[
 155:  K_C=\frac{2^{\sigma_0-1}}{\sigma_0-1}b_C.
 156: \]
 157: 
 158: \begin{theoremC}[Critical renewal and stable law]
 159: The critical letter law satisfies
 160: \[
 161:  \Pr\{C>x\}\sim K_Cx^{1-\sigma_0}.
 162: \]
 163: The associated renewal sequence has the second-order expansion
 164: \[
 165:  u_j(\sigma_0)=\frac1{\mu_C}
 166:  +\frac{K_C}{\mu_C^2(\sigma_0-2)}j^{2-\sigma_0}
 167:  +o(j^{2-\sigma_0}),
 168: \]
 169: and hence
 170: \[
 171:  Z_m^R(-\sigma_0)=\frac{2m}{\mu_C}
 172:  +\frac{2K_C}{\mu_C^2(\sigma_0-2)(3-\sigma_0)}
 173:   m^{3-\sigma_0}+o(m^{3-\sigma_0}).
 174: \]
 175: Moreover, centered sums of independent copies of \(C\), normalized by a
 176: sequence \(a_n\) with \(n\Pr\{C>a_n\}\to1\), converge to the centered
 177: spectrally positive \(\alpha\)-stable law specified in
 178: \Cref{thm:arithmetic-critical-stable-renewal}.  One may take explicitly
 179: \[
 180:  a_n=\left(\frac{2^{\sigma_0+2}}{\sigma_0-1}n\right)^{1/(\sigma_0-1)}.

  80: \((1)\) are endpoint exceptions.  Accounting for those exceptions is also
  81: what resolves the constant in the earlier scalar calculation.

  83: Let
  84: \[
  85:  Z_n(s)=\sum_{\substack{a_1+\cdots+a_r=n\\a_i\ge1,\ a_r\ge2}}
  86:        \mathcal K(a_1,\ldots,a_r)^{-s}.
  87: \]
  88: Dushistova's notation is
  89: \(\sigma_\beta(n)=Z_n(2\beta)\).  The earlier paper obtains a fuller
  90: expansion whose displayed leading coefficient is
  91: \(R_s+2R_s^2\), where \(R_s=\rho_s\)
  92: \cite{Dushistova2007}.  The exact endpoint decomposition gives the following
  93: result.
  94: 
  95: \begin{theoremB}[Context constant and denominator layer]
  96: For every fixed \(s>2\),
  97: \[
  98:  Z_n(s)\sim2\rho_s^2n^{-s}.
  99: \]
 100: Under the termwise change from regular to negative continued fractions, the
 101: cost-\(2d+1\) denominator layer satisfies
 102: \[
 103:  b_{2d+1}(s)=Z_{d+1}(s)\sim2\rho_s^2d^{-s}.
 104: \]
 105: The constant \(2R_s^2\) corrects the leading coefficient in Dushistova's
 106: expansion.  No conclusion is drawn here about its other coefficients.
 107: \end{theoremB}
 108: 
 109: The proof is deliberately local.  The left context of digit sum zero
 110: contributes \(R_s\), the context of digit sum one contributes another
 111: \(R_s\), and the contexts of larger digit sum contribute
 112: \(2(R_s-1)R_s\).  Their sum is \(2R_s^2\).  In the printed convolution the
 113: condition excluding the empty left context is lost after the \(u=1\) term is
 114: separated; because the remaining convolution is doubled, that empty context
 115: is counted twice.  Restoring one subtraction removes precisely the excess
 116: \(R_s\).  This is not a change of continued-fraction convention or exponent.
 117: 
 118: For the probabilistic theorem, the central estimate is a summable
 119: two-context domination.  Once a digit \(a>n/2\) is singled out,
 120: multilinearity of the continuant gives
 121: \[
 122:  \mathcal K(\mathbf u,a,\mathbf v)
 123:  =a\mathcal K(\mathbf u)\mathcal K(\mathbf v)+O_{\mathbf u,\mathbf v}(1).
 124: \]
 125: The inverse-continuant mass of the two contexts is summable for \(s>2\).
 126: Words with no digit above \(n/2\) are negligible: a greedy block
 127: decomposition handles words made only of moderate digits, and a direct cut
 128: handles the cases with one or at least two larger digits.  Dominated
 129: convergence gives the product context law, and the discrete Scheff\'e
 130: argument upgrades pointwise convergence to total variation.
 131: 
 132: The second part of the paper transfers only the scalar layer estimate, not
 133: the total-variation theorem.  Let \(F_0=0,F_1=1\), and let \(R(N)\) count
 134: representations of \(N\) as a sum of distinct members of
 135: \(F_2,F_3,\ldots\).  On the standard Fibonacci layer
 136: \[
 137:  \mathcal I_m=[F_{m+1}-1,F_{m+2}-1),
 138: \]
 139: put
 140: \[
 141:  Z_m^R(t)=\sum_{N\in\mathcal I_m}R(N)^t.
 142: \]
 143: The free-generator parameterization yields a renewal sequence \(u_j(s)\)
 144: whose exact layer identity is
 145: \[
 146:  Z_m^R(-s)=2\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}u_j(s)+u_m(s)-1.
 147: \]
 148: This identity and its proof are given in
 149: \Cref{prop:weighted-generator-renewal}.
 150: 
 151: At \(s=\sigma_0\), let \(C\) be the generator-letter cost.  Set
 152: \(\alpha=\sigma_0-1\in(1,2)\), and let \(\mu_C=\EE C\).  The arithmetic
 153: constant from the two context masses is \(b_C=8\), while
 154: \[
 155:  K_C=\frac{2^{\sigma_0-1}}{\sigma_0-1}b_C.
 156: \]
 157: 
 158: \begin{theoremC}[Critical renewal and stable law]
 159: The critical letter law satisfies
 160: \[
 161:  \Pr\{C>x\}\sim K_Cx^{1-\sigma_0}.
 162: \]
 163: The associated renewal sequence has the second-order expansion
 164: \[
 165:  u_j(\sigma_0)=\frac1{\mu_C}
 166:  +\frac{K_C}{\mu_C^2(\sigma_0-2)}j^{2-\sigma_0}
 167:  +o(j^{2-\sigma_0}),
 168: \]
 169: and hence
 170: \[
 171:  Z_m^R(-\sigma_0)=\frac{2m}{\mu_C}
 172:  +\frac{2K_C}{\mu_C^2(\sigma_0-2)(3-\sigma_0)}
 173:   m^{3-\sigma_0}+o(m^{3-\sigma_0}).
 174: \]
 175: Moreover, centered sums of independent copies of \(C\), normalized by a
 176: sequence \(a_n\) with \(n\Pr\{C>a_n\}\to1\), converge to the centered
 177: spectrally positive \(\alpha\)-stable law specified in
 178: \Cref{thm:arithmetic-critical-stable-renewal}.  One may take explicitly
 179: \[
 180:  a_n=\left(\frac{2^{\sigma_0+2}}{\sigma_0-1}n\right)^{1/(\sigma_0-1)}.

   1: \section{The Fibonacci generator renewal}\label{sec:fibonacci-renewal}
   2: 
   3: Let \(F_0=0,F_1=1\), and \(F_{j+2}=F_{j+1}+F_j\).  The classical Fibonacci
   4: partition function is
   5: \begin{equation}
   6:  R(N):=\card{\left\{(\varepsilon_j)_{j\ge0}\in\{0,1\}^{(\mathbb N)}:
   7:  N=\sum_{j\ge0}\varepsilon_jF_{j+2}\right\}},
   8:  \qquad N\ge0.
   9:  \label{eq:fibonacci-partition-function}
  10: \end{equation}
  11: Thus the available parts are \(1,2,3,5,\ldots\), each used at most once.
  12: For \(m\ge1\), put
  13: \begin{equation}
  14:  \mathcal I_m=[F_{m+1}-1,F_{m+2}-1)\cap\ZZ,
  15:  \qquad
  16:  Z_m^R(t):=\sum_{N\in\mathcal I_m}R(N)^t.
  17:  \label{eq:standard-fibonacci-layer}
  18: \end{equation}
  19: 
  20: We use the generator coordinates of Weinstein
  21: \cite[Secs.~3--5]{Weinstein2016}.  His Fibonacci sequence
  22: \(f_0=f_1=1\) satisfies \(f_j=F_{j+1}\), so his half-open layer
  23: \([f_m-1,f_{m+1}-1)\) is exactly \(\mathcal I_m\), and his partition
  24: function is \(R\) in \eqref{eq:fibonacci-partition-function}.  We record the
  25: part of the parameterization needed for weighted sums.
  26: 
  27: Let
  28: \[
  29:  \mathcal A=\{p/q:0<p<q,\ (p,q)=1\}.
  30: \]
  31: For \(p/q\in\mathcal A\), write its unique negative continued fraction as
  32: \[
  33:  \frac pq=
  34:  \cfrac1{e_1-\cfrac1{e_2-\ddots-\cfrac1{e_k}}},
  35:  \qquad e_i\ge2,
  36: \]
  37: and recall from \Cref{sec:brocot-condensation} that
  38: \[
  39:  d(p/q)=\sum_{i=1}^k(e_i-1),
  40:  \qquad c(p/q)=2d(p/q)+1.
  41: \]
  42: For real \(s\), define
  43: \begin{equation}
  44:  b_\ell(s)=\sum_{\substack{p/q\in\mathcal A\\c(p/q)=\ell}}q^{-s},
  45:  \qquad
  46:  B_s(z)=\sum_{\ell\ge1}b_\ell(s)z^\ell.
  47:  \label{eq:renewal-letter-series}
  48: \end{equation}
  49: Only odd costs occur.  The smallest costs are \(3\) and \(5\), represented
  50: by \(1/2\) and \(1/3\).
  51: 
  52: Put
  53: \begin{equation}
  54:  u_0(s)=1,
  55:  \qquad
  56:  u_j(s)=\sum_{\ell=1}^jb_\ell(s)u_{j-\ell}(s)\quad(j\ge1).
  57:  \label{eq:weighted-renewal-coefficients}
  58: \end{equation}
  59: Equivalently, in formal power series,
  60: \begin{equation}
  61:  U_s(z):=\sum_{j\ge0}u_j(s)z^j=\frac1{1-B_s(z)}.
  62:  \label{eq:weighted-renewal-generating-function}
  63: \end{equation}
  64: 
  65: It is useful to retain the coefficient form of this formal identity.  For a
  66: word \(w=(x_1,\ldots,x_r)\in\mathcal A^r\), with
  67: \(x_i=p_i/q_i\), set
  68: \[
  69:  c(w)=\sum_{i=1}^r c(x_i),\qquad
  70:  Q(w)=\prod_{i=1}^r q_i,
  71: \]
  72: and give the empty word cost zero and weight one.
  73: 
  74: \begin{proposition}[Weighted generator renewal]
  75: \label{prop:weighted-generator-renewal}
  76: For every real \(s\) and \(m\ge1\),
  77: \begin{equation}
  78:  Z_m^R(-s)
  79:  =1+u_m(s)+2\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}u_j(s)
  80:  =2\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}u_j(s)+u_m(s)-1.
  81:  \label{eq:standard-layer-renewal}
  82: \end{equation}
  83: Moreover, \(u_j(s)\) is exactly the total inverse-\(s\) weight of the
  84: Weinstein generators of cost \(j\).
  85: \end{proposition}
  86: 
  87: \begin{proof}
  88: Under Weinstein's free-monoid bijection, a generating number \(g\)
  89: corresponds uniquely to a nonempty word
  90: \[
  91:  w=\frac{p_1}{q_1}\times\cdots\times\frac{p_r}{q_r}
  92:  \qquad(p_i/q_i\in\mathcal A).
  93: \]
  94: Proposition~3.3 and formula~(16) of
  95: \cite{Weinstein2016}, translated through the preceding Fibonacci convention,
  96: give
  97: \begin{equation}
  98:  R(g)=\prod_{i=1}^rq_i,
  99:  \qquad
 100:  L(g)=\sum_{i=1}^rc(p_i/q_i),
 101:  \label{eq:generator-weight-cost}
 102: \end{equation}
 103: where \(L(g)\) is the largest Zeckendorf index of \(g\).  Unique
 104: factorization in the free monoid and the Cauchy products in
 105: \eqref{eq:weighted-renewal-generating-function} show that the total weight
 106: \(R(g)^{-s}\) of generators with \(L(g)=j\) is \(u_j(s)\).  Conversely
 107: every nonempty letter word gives one generator; the empty word supplies
 108: \(u_0(s)=1\) and is not a generating number.
 109: 
 110: It remains to count one generating orbit in a fixed half-open Fibonacci
 111: layer.  Weinstein's normal form consists of the four branches
 112: \([a]g,[a]\tau g,\sigma([a]g),\sigma([a]\tau g)\), with \(a\ge0\).  His
 113: formulas~(17)--(19) give the corresponding largest indices
 114: \[
 115:  \begin{array}{c|c}
 116:  \text{branch}&\text{largest index}\ \\ \hline
 117:  {}[a]g&L(g)+2a\\
 118:  {}[a]\tau g&L(g)+1+2a\\
 119:  \sigma({}[a]g)&L(g)+1+2a\\
 120:  \sigma({}[a]\tau g)&L(g)+2+2a.
 121:  \end{array}
 122: \]
 123: The two middle entries are distinct because the action is free.  Hence an
 124: orbit of generator cost \(j=L(g)\) contributes one point to
 125: \(\mathcal I_j\), two points to every \(\mathcal I_m\) with \(m>j\), and
 126: no point to earlier layers.  Distinct generators have disjoint orbits.
 127: There is in addition exactly one partition of multiplicity \(1\) in each
 128: \(\mathcal I_m\), namely its left endpoint.  Summing the orbit weights
 129: first over all generators of a fixed cost and then over the admissible costs
 130: therefore gives
 131: \[
 132:  Z_m^R(-s)
 133:  =1+\sum_{\substack{g\ {\rm generating}\\L(g)=m}}R(g)^{-s}
 134:   +2\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}
 135:     \sum_{\substack{g\ {\rm generating}\\L(g)=j}}R(g)^{-s}.
 136: \]
 137: The coefficient identity changes this one-orbit layer count into
 138: \(1+u_m(s)+2\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}u_j(s)\).  Finally, inserting
 139: \(u_0(s)=1\) gives the standard partial-renewal-sum form on the right of
 140: \eqref{eq:standard-layer-renewal}.
 141: \end{proof}
 142: 
 143: \begin{corollary}[Layer generating function and adjacent layers]
 144: \label{cor:fibonacci-layer-generating-function}
 145: As a formal power series in \(z\),
 146: \begin{equation}
 147:  \sum_{m\ge1}Z_m^R(-s)z^m
 148:  =\frac{(1+z)U_s(z)-1}{1-z}
 149:  =\frac{1+z}{(1-z)(1-B_s(z))}-\frac1{1-z}.
 150:  \label{eq:fibonacci-layer-generating-function}
 151: \end{equation}
 152: Equivalently, the change between two adjacent standard Fibonacci layers is
 153: \begin{equation}
 154:  Z_{m+1}^R(-s)-Z_m^R(-s)=u_m(s)+u_{m+1}(s),
 155:  \qquad m\ge1.
 156:  \label{eq:adjacent-fibonacci-layer-increment}
 157: \end{equation}
 158: \end{corollary}
 159: 
 160: \begin{proof}
 161: Multiply the second identity in \eqref{eq:standard-layer-renewal} by \(z^m\)
 162: and sum over \(m\ge1\).  Formal rearrangement gives
 163: \begin{align*}
 164:  \sum_{m\ge1}Z_m^R(-s)z^m
 165:  &=\frac{2z}{1-z}U_s(z)+U_s(z)-1-\frac z{1-z}\\
 166:  &=\frac{(1+z)U_s(z)-1}{1-z}.
 167: \end{align*}
 168: Inserting \eqref{eq:weighted-renewal-generating-function} proves the second
 169: form in \eqref{eq:fibonacci-layer-generating-function}.  Subtracting
 170: \eqref{eq:standard-layer-renewal} at \(m\) from the same identity at
 171: \(m+1\) gives \eqref{eq:adjacent-fibonacci-layer-increment}.  Thus the
 172: standard layer sum, its discrete layer derivative, and the renewal
 173: generating function are exact transforms of one another.
 174: \end{proof}

 241: \(O(\varphi(q)(\log q)^2)\).  Therefore
 242: \[
 243:  \mu_C\ll\sum_{q\ge2}q^{1-\sigma_0}(\log q)^2<\infty.
 244: \]
 245: This verifies the finite-mean hypothesis used in the critical renewal
 246: argument below.
 247: 
 248: Combining the normalization with
 249: \Cref{lem:letter-word-renewal-coefficients,prop:weighted-generator-renewal}
 250: puts the identity needed in the next section in purely probabilistic form.
 251: If \(S_0=0\) and \(S_r=C_1+\cdots+C_r\), then
 252: \begin{equation}
 253: \begin{aligned}
 254:  u_j(\sigma_0)&=\sum_{r\ge0}\Pr\{S_r=j\},\\
 255:  Z_m^R(-\sigma_0)
 256:  &=2\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}u_j(\sigma_0)+u_m(\sigma_0)-1\\
 257:  &=2\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}\sum_{r\ge0}\Pr\{S_r=j\}
 258:   +\sum_{r\ge0}\Pr\{S_r=m\}-1.
 259: \end{aligned}
 260:  \label{eq:critical-weighted-renewal-identity}
 261: \end{equation}
 262: Thus the Fibonacci layer is not merely comparable with a renewal sum: it
 263: is exactly a partial sum of the renewal mass, with the terminal renewal mass
 264: and the multiplicity-one endpoint displayed separately.
 265: 
 266: There is also a pathwise form of the same identity.  Since every increment
 267: is at least three, the random count
 268: \[
 269:  \mathcal N_m:=\card{\{r\ge0:S_r<m\}}
 270: \]
 271: is finite.  Tonelli's theorem and strict increase of \(S_r\) give
 272: \begin{align*}
 273:  \EE\mathcal N_m
 274:  &=\sum_{r\ge0}\Pr\{S_r<m\}
 275:  =\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}u_j(\sigma_0),\\
 276:  \Pr\{\text{\(m\) is a renewal epoch}\}
 277:  &=u_m(\sigma_0).
 278: \end{align*}
 279: Consequently,
 280: \begin{equation}
 281:  Z_m^R(-\sigma_0)
 282:  =2\EE\mathcal N_m
 283:   +\Pr\{\text{\(m\) is a renewal epoch}\}-1.
 284:  \label{eq:critical-layer-renewal-count}
 285: \end{equation}
 286: The factor two is the pair of noninitial orbit branches, the probability
 287: term is the single branch entering at layer \(m\), and the subtraction
 288: removes the extra copy of the multiplicity-one endpoint introduced by
 289: \(u_0=1\).  This identifies each term of the weighted layer formula before
 290: any asymptotic renewal theorem is applied.

#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""Tests for verify_critical_constants.py."""

from __future__ import annotations

import unittest

import mpmath as mp

from verify_critical_constants import (
    critical_exponent,
    exact_constants,
    truncated_context_constants,
)


class CriticalConstantsTest(unittest.TestCase):
    @classmethod
    def setUpClass(cls) -> None:
        cls.sigma = critical_exponent()

    def test_defining_zeta_equation_and_range(self) -> None:
        residual = mp.zeta(self.sigma - 1) / mp.zeta(self.sigma) - 2
        self.assertLess(abs(residual), mp.mpf("1e-40"))
        self.assertGreater(self.sigma, 2)
        self.assertLess(self.sigma, 3)

    def test_closed_form_constants(self) -> None:
        alpha, exponent, k_c, scale = exact_constants(self.sigma)
        self.assertAlmostEqual(float(alpha), 1.4787507857339603, places=14)
        self.assertAlmostEqual(float(exponent), 0.5212492142660397, places=14)
        self.assertAlmostEqual(float(k_c), 15.07798619025016, places=13)
        self.assertAlmostEqual(float(scale), 6.2639823573126, places=13)

    def test_totient_sums_approach_context_constant_from_below(self) -> None:
        values = truncated_context_constants(self.sigma, (1_000, 10_000, 100_000))
        sequence = list(values.values())
        self.assertEqual(sequence, sorted(sequence))
        self.assertGreater(sequence[-1], 7.95)
        self.assertLess(sequence[-1], 8.0)


if __name__ == "__main__":
    unittest.main()

#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""Reproduce the numerical consistency check for the critical constants."""

from __future__ import annotations

import argparse
from array import array

import mpmath as mp


DEFAULT_CUTOFFS = (1_000, 10_000, 100_000, 1_000_000)


def critical_exponent(decimal_places: int = 50) -> mp.mpf:
    """Solve zeta(s - 1) / zeta(s) = 2 on (2, 3)."""
    mp.mp.dps = decimal_places
    return mp.findroot(lambda s: mp.zeta(s - 1) / mp.zeta(s) - 2, (mp.mpf("2.4"), mp.mpf("2.6")))


def totients(limit: int) -> array:
    """Return phi(0), ..., phi(limit) using an integer sieve."""
    values = array("I", range(limit + 1))
    for prime in range(2, limit + 1):
        if values[prime] == prime:
            for multiple in range(prime, limit + 1, prime):
                values[multiple] -= values[multiple] // prime
    return values


def truncated_context_constants(sigma: mp.mpf, cutoffs: tuple[int, ...]) -> dict[int, float]:
    """Compute 2 rho_Q^2 at the requested totient-series cutoffs."""
    if not cutoffs or min(cutoffs) < 2:
        raise ValueError("cutoffs must be integers at least 2")
    ordered = tuple(sorted(set(cutoffs)))
    phi = totients(ordered[-1])
    sigma_float = float(sigma)
    rho_q = 1.0
    results: dict[int, float] = {}
    next_index = 0
    for q in range(2, ordered[-1] + 1):
        rho_q += phi[q] / q**sigma_float
        if q == ordered[next_index]:
            results[q] = 2.0 * rho_q * rho_q
            next_index += 1
            if next_index == len(ordered):
                break
    return results


def exact_constants(sigma: mp.mpf) -> tuple[mp.mpf, mp.mpf, mp.mpf, mp.mpf]:
    """Return alpha, correction exponent, K_C, and the scale coefficient."""
    alpha = sigma - 1
    correction_exponent = 3 - sigma
    k_c = mp.power(2, sigma + 2) / (sigma - 1)
    scale_coefficient = mp.power(k_c, 1 / alpha)
    return alpha, correction_exponent, k_c, scale_coefficient


def main() -> None:
    parser = argparse.ArgumentParser(description=__doc__)
    parser.add_argument(
        "--max-cutoff",
        type=int,
        default=DEFAULT_CUTOFFS[-1],
        help="largest totient-series cutoff (default: 1000000)",
    )
    args = parser.parse_args()
    cutoffs = tuple(q for q in DEFAULT_CUTOFFS if q <= args.max_cutoff)
    if not cutoffs or cutoffs[-1] != args.max_cutoff:
        cutoffs = (*cutoffs, args.max_cutoff)

    sigma = critical_exponent()
    alpha, correction_exponent, k_c, scale_coefficient = exact_constants(sigma)
    approximations = truncated_context_constants(sigma, cutoffs)

    print(f"sigma_0 = {mp.nstr(sigma, 17)}")
    print(f"alpha = sigma_0 - 1 = {mp.nstr(alpha, 17)}")
    print(f"3 - sigma_0 = {mp.nstr(correction_exponent, 17)}")
    print(f"b_C = 8 (exact)")
    print(f"K_C = {mp.nstr(k_c, 17)}")
    print(
        "a_n coefficient in a_n = coefficient * n^(1/alpha): "
        f"{mp.nstr(scale_coefficient, 17)}"
    )
    print("truncated checks 2 * rho_Q^2:")
    for cutoff, value in approximations.items():
        print(f"  Q={cutoff:>7d}: {value:.10f}")


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

\title[Brocot condensation and critical renewal]{Brocot Condensation and\
Critical Fibonacci Renewal}

\author{Haobo Ma}
\address{AELF PTE LTD., \#14-02, Marina Bay Financial Centre Tower 1,
8 Marina Blvd, Singapore 018981, Singapore}
\email{auric@aelf.io}

\hypersetup{
  pdftitle={Brocot Condensation and Critical Fibonacci Renewal},
  pdfauthor={Haobo Ma and Wenlin Zhang},
  pdfsubject={Denominator-weighted Brocot fractions and critical Fibonacci renewal},
  pdfkeywords={Brocot fractions, continued fractions, continuants, condensation, Fibonacci partition function, renewal theory}
}
\date{}
\subjclass[2020]{11A55, 11B39, 60F05, 60K05}
\keywords{Brocot fractions, continued fractions, continuants, condensation,
Fibonacci partition function, renewal theory, stable laws}

\begin{document}

\begin{abstract}
Fix \(s>2\), and weight the canonical regular continued fractions
\([0;a_1,\ldots,a_r]\) with \(a_r\ge2\) and
\(a_1+\cdots+a_r=n\) proportionally to the inverse \(s\)-th power of their
denominators.  We prove a total-variation condensation theorem.  With
probability tending to one there is a unique partial quotient larger than
\(n/2\), and the words on its two sides converge jointly to independent
finite words with inverse-continuant laws.  This gives the exact limiting
defect and location laws and an asymptotic factorization of the denominator.

The normalization is the fixed-digit-sum continuant sum studied by
Dushistova after the Farey-tree work of Moshchevitin and Zhigljavsky.  We
prove
\[
 Z_n(s)\sim
 2\left\{\frac{\zeta(s-1)}{\zeta(s)}\right\}^{\!2}n^{-s}
\]
without a tail hypothesis.  An exact context calculation identifies an
endpoint overcount in Dushistova's argument and changes its printed leading
coefficient from \(R_s+2R_s^2\) to \(2R_s^2\); no assertion is made about the
remaining coefficients of that expansion.

At the arithmetic critical point
\(\zeta(\sigma_0-1)/\zeta(\sigma_0)=2\), the scalar estimate gives an exact
regularly varying cost tail with context constant \(8\).  Combining it with
the free-generator coordinates of Weinstein yields a weighted renewal
identity for the classical Fibonacci partition function, a spectrally
positive stable domain of attraction, and the noninteger
\(m^{3-\sigma_0}\) correction to its critical one-layer partition sum.
\end{abstract}

BROCOT CONDENSATION AND CRITICAL
                FIBONACCI RENEWAL

HAOBO MA AND WENLIN ZHANG

Abstract. Fix s > 2, and weight the canonical regular continued
fractions [0; a1, . . . , ar] with ar  2 and a1 + З З З + ar = n propor-
tionally to the inverse s-th power of their denominators. We prove
a total-variation condensation theorem. With probability tending
to one there is a unique partial quotient larger than n/2, and the
words on its two sides converge jointly to independent finite words
with inverse-continuant laws. This gives the exact limiting defect
and location laws and an asymptotic factorization of the denomi-
nator.

   The normalization is the fixed-digit-sum continuant sum stud-
ied by Dushistova after the Farey-tree work of Moshchevitin and
Zhigljavsky. We prove

Zn(s)  2  (s - 1)  2

           (s)      n-s

without a tail hypothesis. An exact context calculation identifies
an endpoint overcount in Dushistova's argument and changes its
printed leading coefficient from Rs + 2Rs2 to 2Rs2; no assertion is
made about the remaining coefficients of that expansion.

   At the arithmetic critical point (0 - 1)/(0) = 2, the scalar
estimate gives an exact regularly varying cost tail with context
constant 8. Combining it with the free-generator coordinates of
Weinstein yields a weighted renewal identity for the classical Fi-
bonacci partition function, a spectrally positive stable domain of
attraction, and the noninteger m3-0 correction to its critical one-
layer partition sum.

                                 1. Introduction

   The Stern­Brocot and Farey constructions organize rational num-
bers by continued-fraction complexity. A natural statistical question
is what a typical rational looks like when a fixed complexity layer is
biased toward small denominators. In the model considered here, the

   2020 Mathematics Subject Classification. 11A55, 11B39, 60F05, 60K05.
   Key words and phrases. Brocot fractions, continued fractions, continuants, con-
densation, Fibonacci partition function, renewal theory, stable laws.

                                                           1
2  HAOBO MA AND WENLIN ZHANG

complexity of the canonical regular continued fraction

                    x = [0; a1, . . . , ar], ai  1, ar  2,

is the digit sum a1 + З З З + ar = n, and the weight is den(x)-s for a
fixed s > 2. The continuant in the denominator is nonmultiplicative
across the digits. Nevertheless, a single partial quotient absorbs all but
a tight amount of the digit sum, while the two finite contexts on its
sides converge to independent arithmetic laws.

   This condensation statement is stronger than the scalar asymptotic
for the normalizing sum. It identifies the limiting context words in
total variation, the defect of the largest partial quotient, its position
in the continued fraction, the numbers of digits on either side, and the
leading factorization of the sampled denominator. The proof also gives
the scalar asymptotic with its exact constant. That scalar consequence
becomes the arithmetic input for a different but closely linked ques-
tion: the critical renewal structure of the classical Fibonacci partition
function.

   The link is supplied by Weinstein's free generators for Fibonacci
partitions [11]. Their letters are reduced fractions in (0, 1); a letter p/q
carries multiplicative weight q and an odd cost read from its negative
continued fraction. The cost-2d + 1 letter sum is termwise identical to
the regular-continuant sum at digit sum d + 1. Thus the same scalar
estimate that normalizes the condensation law determines the critical
generator-cost tail. At the unique 0 > 2 satisfying

                                    (0 - 1) = 2,
                                       (0 )

the generator letters form a probability distribution with a regularly
varying tail of index 1 - 0. Its mean is finite and its variance is infi-
nite. A second-order lattice renewal theorem then produces a fractional
power correction to the critical Fibonacci partition sum, and the same
tail places the cost in the domain of attraction of a spectrally positive
stable law.

1.1. Main results. For a finite word w, let K(w) be its regular con-
tinuant and let |w|1 be its digit sum. Write WL for all finite positive
words, including the empty word, and WR for the canonical regular
words, again including the empty word. Put

        (s - 1)
   s =          .
         (s)

The precise definitions occur in Section 2. The first result is summa-
rized as follows.
         BROCOT CONDENSATION AND CRITICAL RENEWAL                   3

Theorem A (Total-variation condensation). For fixed s > 2, sample
a canonical continued fraction of digit sum n with probability propor-
tional to the inverse s-th power of its denominator. With probability
tending to one it has a unique digit larger than n/2. If Un, Vn are the
words to the left and right of that digit, then

                      (Un, Vn) -T-V (U, V ),

where U, V are independent and

         Pr{U = u} =  K(u)-s     ,   Pr{V = v} =  K(v)-s      .

                      2s                                   s

The defect, the location data, and the normalized denominator con-

verge as stated in Theorem 2.1.

The asymmetry between the two context laws is caused only by the

canonical terminal-digit convention. A regular rational has two positive

continued-fraction expansions, whereas the canonical form retains the

one whose final digit is at least two. The empty word and the one-letter

word (1) are endpoint exceptions. Accounting for those exceptions is

also what resolves the constant in the earlier scalar calculation.

Let

         Zn(s) =                     K(a1, . . . , ar)-s.

                      a1 +ЗЗЗ+ar =n
                      ai1, ar2

Dushistova's notation is (n) = Zn(2). The earlier paper obtains a
fuller expansion whose displayed leading coefficient is Rs + 2Rs2, where
Rs = s [4]. The exact endpoint decomposition gives the following

result.

Theorem B (Context constant and denominator layer). For every
fixed s > 2,

                                   Zn(s)  2s2n-s.
Under the termwise change from regular to negative continued frac-
tions, the cost-2d + 1 denominator layer satisfies

                          b2d+1(s) = Zd+1(s)  2s2d-s.

The constant 2Rs2 corrects the leading coefficient in Dushistova's ex-
pansion. No conclusion is drawn here about its other coefficients.

   The proof is deliberately local. The left context of digit sum zero
contributes Rs, the context of digit sum one contributes another Rs,
and the contexts of larger digit sum contribute 2(Rs - 1)Rs. Their
sum is 2Rs2. In the printed convolution the condition excluding the
empty left context is lost after the u = 1 term is separated; because
4                   HAOBO MA AND WENLIN ZHANG

the remaining convolution is doubled, that empty context is counted
twice. Restoring one subtraction removes precisely the excess Rs. This
is not a change of continued-fraction convention or exponent.

   For the probabilistic theorem, the central estimate is a summable
two-context domination. Once a digit a > n/2 is singled out, multilin-
earity of the continuant gives

                      K(u, a, v) = aK(u)K(v) + Ou,v(1).

The inverse-continuant mass of the two contexts is summable for s > 2.
Words with no digit above n/2 are negligible: a greedy block decom-
position handles words made only of moderate digits, and a direct cut
handles the cases with one or at least two larger digits. Dominated
convergence gives the product context law, and the discrete Scheffщ
argument upgrades pointwise convergence to total variation.

   The second part of the paper transfers only the scalar layer estimate,
not the total-variation theorem. Let F0 = 0, F1 = 1, and let R(N )
count representations of N as a sum of distinct members of F2, F3, . . ..
On the standard Fibonacci layer

                    Im = [Fm+1 - 1, Fm+2 - 1),

put

                       ZmR(t) =          R(N )t.

                                   N Im

The free-generator parameterization yields a renewal sequence uj(s)
whose exact layer identity is

                                          m-1

                  ZmR(-s) = 2 uj(s) + um(s) - 1.

                                          j=0

This identity and its proof are given in Proposition 3.1.

   The noninteger power is a finite-size signature of the arithmetic crit-
ical tail. Regular variation is not assumed in the renewal step: it is
proved first from the denominator-layer asymptotic. The second-order
renewal theorem of Omey and Van Gulck [8] then applies because the
law is aperiodic, supported on the positive integers, and has finite mean.
The stable conclusion follows from the standard one-sided domain-of-
attraction criterion [5].

1.2. History and contribution boundary. Moshchevitin and Zhigl-
javsky studied entropy sums associated with partitions of the unit in-
terval generated by the Farey tree [7]. Dushistova developed precise
fixed-digit-sum denominator estimates for Brocot sequences [4]. The
present scalar sum is exactly her local sum after the exponent change
s = 2, and the relation is termwise rather than asymptotic. The
contribution here is the joint total-variation structure, the endpoint
calculation of the leading constant, and the shorter scalar proof that
does not assume an auxiliary tail estimate. The correction is confined
to the leading coefficient: the arguments here neither verify nor reject
the later coefficients in the published expansion.

   Condensation under a large-sum conditioning is familiar for indepen-
dent heavy-tailed variables and for probabilistic combinatorial struc-
tures. In particular, total-variation one-big-jump results and Gibbs-
partition condensation provide useful comparisons [1, 10]. They do
not imply the theorem here. A continuant weight does not factor into
weights of its digits, so the model is not an independent allocation or
a multiplicative Gibbs partition. The proof instead exploits the exact
two-sided continuant inequality and the summability of all finite con-
texts. Accordingly, the result is presented as a structural theorem for
this nonmultiplicative arithmetic family, not as a general condensation
principle.
6  HAOBO MA AND WENLIN ZHANG

   Fibonacci representations have a long history, beginning with the
canonical representation theorem and continuing through exact formu-
las and extremal questions [12, 3, 2]. Weinstein's orbit and generator
theory gives the coordinate system used here [11]. We do not repro-
duce that classification. We state the precise pieces of it needed for the
weighted layer count, check the Fibonacci indexing and endpoint con-
vention, and derive the renewal identity. The critical tail constant, the
second-order renewal term, and the stable limit are then consequences
of the continuant calculation in this paper.

   A companion manuscript by the same authors, Finite-window Zeck-
endorf thermodynamics, studies an exact two-layer finite-window cor-
respondence, critical coexistence, and large deviations across the pres-
sure corner. No result from that manuscript is used here. Conversely,
its leading critical renewal law requires only the finite-mean renewal
theorem; the exact tail and fractional correction proved here are not
prerequisites for its principal finite-window results.

1.3. Organization. Section 2 defines the denominator-weighted Bro-
cot model, proves the context estimates and the total-variation theo-
rem, and derives the corrected denominator-layer asymptotic. Section 3
introduces the classical Fibonacci partition function, the minimal We-
instein generator coordinates, and the exact one-layer weighted renewal
identity. The arithmetic critical tail, second-order renewal expansion,
stable law, and Fibonacci finite-size consequence are proved in Sec-
tion 4. The final section records the scope of the two mechanisms and
the open directions left by the argument.

   The theorem concerns canonical continued fractions, their denomi-

s(h) :=                               K(u)-s

                               uWL
                               |u|1h

Gs(N, h)    1   2s                    s(h)N/(2h)-1      .  (2.15)

Proof. Scan a word from the left and close a block at the first digit

for which the block's digit sum reaches h; repeat, leaving the final

remainder unclosed. This factorization is unique. Since every digit is

smaller than h, each closed block has digit sum in [h, 2h), and a word

of digit sum at least N has at least N/(2h) - 1 closed blocks. The

concatenation inequality makes the inverse-continuant weight at most

the product of the block weights. Enlarge each closed-block class to all

words of digit sum at least h, of mass s(h), and the remainder class to

all of WL, of mass 2s. Summing the resulting geometric series proves

the bound; s(h)  0 by (2.12).

pairs carrying limiting probability at least 1 - . On that finite set

the last convergence is uniform, and a = n - |u|1 - |v|1  . The
complement has limsup probability at most , apart from the vanishing

noncondensed event. Letting   0 proves (2.8).

    q=2

respectively

          Q = 103 : 7.6323590814,             Q = 104 : 7.8769320348,
          Q = 105 : 7.9590248673,             Q = 106 : 7.9863810907,

consistent with the proved limit bC = 8. The computation is only a
sanity check; the proof of the constant is the exact context evaluation
in Lemma 4.2. The verification script and its unit test are included in
the artifacts directory.

                                  5. Conclusion

   The denominator-weighted Brocot layer exhibits a complete one-
digit condensation picture for every fixed s > 2. Its nonmultiplicative
continuant weight does not place it under a general independent-sum
BROCOT CONDENSATION AND CRITICAL RENEWAL  25

or Gibbs partition theorem. The arithmetic replacement is the two-
sided context estimate: after the large digit is removed, the inverse-
continuant masses of the left and right words are summable and the
central continuant is asymptotically their product times the large digit.
This yields the product context law in total variation and all of its de-
fect, location, and denominator consequences. In particular, the result
concerns the entire sampled continued fraction rather than only its
normalizing sum: uniqueness of the large digit, tightness of the dis-
carded digit mass, and convergence of both contexts are simultaneous
conclusions.

   The same proof has a scalar output. It gives the coefficient 2{(s -
1)/(s)}2, identifies the endpoint overcount in the earlier convolution,
and transfers termwise to the odd denominator layers used by Fibonacci
generators. The correction concerns only that leading coefficient. A de-
termination of the remaining coefficients would require uniform expan-
sions beyond the dominated-convergence argument used here. Nothing
in the endpoint calculation verifies, modifies, or rules out those remain-
ing coefficients. This boundary is essential because the condensation
proof uses a first-order central-continuant approximation and summa-
ble domination; it does not produce the uniform higher-order remainder
needed for a full corrected expansion.

   At the arithmetic critical exponent, the scalar output becomes an
exact regularly varying generator-cost law. The free-generator orbit
count then turns the classical Fibonacci layer sum into a renewal sum.
The tail constant bC = 8 controls both the spectrally positive stable
limit and the fractional m3-0 term. This is the full shared mechanism:
continued-fraction contexts determine the critical tail, and renewal the-
ory transfers that tail to the Fibonacci partition function.

   The fractional term is unbounded but sublinear, hence a genuine
finite-size contribution rather than a relabeling of finitely many end-
point orbits.

   Several limitations follow from the proof. The stable statement is a
domain-of-attraction result for sums of generator-letter costs; it is not a
local limit theorem and supplies no rate of convergence. The Fibonacci
expansion is established at the single arithmetic critical exponent and
does not assert an analogous fractional correction away from criticality.
Nor does the argument classify other continued-fraction weights: the
exact constant uses the regular/negative continued-fraction correspon-
dence, the two context masses, and Weinstein's particular free monoid.
Models without those identities require separate arithmetic input.

   The companion manuscript Finite-window Zeckendorf thermody-
namics starts from an exact finite-window correspondence and studies
26  HAOBO MA AND WENLIN ZHANG

local spectra, critical coexistence, and large deviations. Those ques-
tions are logically independent of the total-variation condensation
theorem, and the two arguments are logically autonomous: no re-
sult of the companion manuscript is used here, while its principal
finite-window results do not require the tail constant, stable law, or
fractional correction proved here. Keeping the two settings separate
also makes the scope of the present conclusion explicit. The argument
establishes structural consequences specific to this nonmultiplicative
continuant model and its Fibonacci generator renewal; it does not
establish a new universal mechanism for condensation or freezing.

 150: \begin{proof}
 151: Introduce the equilibrium mass and its tail
 152: \[
 153:  p^{\rm e}_j=\frac{\overline F(j-1)}\mu,
 154:  \qquad
 155:  \overline F^{\rm e}(j)
 156:  =\sum_{k>j}p^{\rm e}_k
 157:  =\frac1\mu\sum_{r\ge j}\overline F(r).
 158: \]
 159: The second-order assertion used here is the first displayed renewal
 160: asymptotic in Omey and Van Gulck
 161: \cite[Sec.~3.2, first assertion following Eq.~(8)]{OmeyVanGulck2015}:
 162: under the three hypotheses above,
 163: \begin{equation}
 164:  v_j-\frac1\mu\sim\frac{\overline F^{\rm e}(j)}\mu.
 165:  \label{eq:omey-vangulck-equilibrium-form}
 166: \end{equation}
 167: Their paper states the result for a tail regularly varying with index
 168: \(-\alpha\), \(\alpha>1\); their \(u_n\), \(\mu\), and
 169: \(\overline F_n\) are respectively \(v_j\), \(\mu\), and
 170: \(\overline F(j)\) here.  The retained \(r=j\) term is
 171: \(O(j^{-\alpha})=o(j^{1-\alpha})\), so it does not change the constant in
 172: discrete Karamata summation:
 173: \[
 174:  \overline F^{\rm e}(j)
 175:  =\frac1\mu\sum_{r\ge j}\overline F(r)
 176:  \sim\frac{K}{\mu(\alpha-1)}j^{1-\alpha}.
 177: \]
 178: Substitution in \eqref{eq:omey-vangulck-equilibrium-form} gives
 179: \eqref{eq:second-order-renewal-input}.  Thus neither the power nor its
 180: coefficient is hidden in the citation.
 181: \end{proof}

 295: Finally, \(n/a_n^2\to0\) because \(\alpha<2\).  Expanding the logarithm
 296: of the characteristic function therefore shows that centering by the full
 297: mean \(n\mu\) produces exactly the displayed exponent.  This proves
 298: \eqref{eq:generic-one-sided-stable-limit} and also shows why no negative
 299: tail term or unspecified scale factor remains.
 300: \end{proof}
 301: 
 302: \begin{proof}[Proof of \Cref{thm:arithmetic-critical-stable-renewal}]
 303: Equation \eqref{eq:critical-renewal-normalization} makes the displayed
 304: letter weights a probability measure.  The local estimate and both tail
 305: constants follow from \Cref{lem:critical-tail-summation}.
 306: \Cref{prop:critical-renewal-hypotheses} gives
 307: \eqref{eq:critical-renewal-second-order} with every lattice-renewal
 308: hypothesis verified.
 309: 
 310: Because \(2-\sigma_0\in(-1,0)\), summation of the renewal remainder is
 311: legitimate: if \(r_j=o(j^{2-\sigma_0})\), then for every
 312: \(\varepsilon>0\),
 313: \[
 314:  \left|\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}r_j\right|
 315:  \le O_\varepsilon(1)+\varepsilon
 316:       \sum_{j=1}^{m-1}j^{2-\sigma_0}
 317:  =o(m^{3-\sigma_0}).
 318: \]
 319: Also,
 320: \[
 321:  \sum_{j=1}^{m-1}j^{2-\sigma_0}
 322:  \sim\frac{m^{3-\sigma_0}}{3-\sigma_0}.
 323: \]
 324: Substitution in the exact identity
 325: \eqref{eq:critical-weighted-renewal-identity} gives
 326: \eqref{eq:standard-layer-critical-second-order}.  The terminal term
 327: \(u_m=O(1)\) and the endpoint \(-1\) are
 328: \(o(m^{3-\sigma_0})\).
 329: 
 330: Finally, take \(X=C\) in \Cref{lem:one-sided-stable-domain}.  The finite
 331: mean was verified in \Cref{prop:critical-renewal-hypotheses}, and
 332: \Cref{lem:critical-tail-summation} supplies its tail with \(K=K_C\).
 333: This proves \eqref{eq:critical-cost-stable-domain} and
 334: \eqref{eq:centered-stable-characteristic-exponent}.  It also gives the
 335: explicit normalization \eqref{eq:explicit-critical-stable-normalization}.
 336: \end{proof}
 337: 
 338: \begin{corollary}[Critical asymptotic for the classical Fibonacci partition function]
 339: \label{cor:classical-fibonacci-critical-asymptotic}
 340: Let \(R(N)\) count the representations of \(N\) as a sum of distinct
 341: members of \(F_2,F_3,\ldots\), as in
 342: \eqref{eq:fibonacci-partition-function}.  Then
 343: \begin{align}
 344:  &\sum_{F_{m+1}-1\le N<F_{m+2}-1}R(N)^{-\sigma_0}
 345:  \notag\\
 346:  &\qquad=\frac{2m}{\mu_C}
 347:  +\frac{2K_C}{\mu_C^2(\sigma_0-2)(3-\sigma_0)}
 348:   m^{3-\sigma_0}+o(m^{3-\sigma_0}).
 349:  \label{eq:classical-fibonacci-critical-asymptotic}
 350: \end{align}
 351: Equivalently, relative to the known linear renewal scale,
 352: \begin{equation}
 353:  \sum_{N\in\mathcal I_m}R(N)^{-\sigma_0}
 354:  =\frac{2m}{\mu_C}\left[
 355:  1+\frac{K_C}{\mu_C(\sigma_0-2)(3-\sigma_0)}
 356:  m^{2-\sigma_0}+o(m^{2-\sigma_0})\right].
 357:  \label{eq:relative-fibonacci-critical-correction}
 358: \end{equation}
 359: The exponent \(3-\sigma_0\) lies strictly between zero and one.  Hence the
 360: second term diverges on the absolute partition-function scale, is smaller
 361: than the leading linear term, and cannot be absorbed into a bounded endpoint
 362: correction.
 363: \end{corollary}
 364: 
 365: \begin{proof}
 366: The interval in \eqref{eq:classical-fibonacci-critical-asymptotic} is
 367: exactly \(\mathcal I_m\), so the left side is
 368: \(Z_m^R(-\sigma_0)\).  Equation
 369: \eqref{eq:standard-layer-critical-second-order} proves the first expansion.
 370: Factoring out \(2m/\mu_C\) gives
 371: \eqref{eq:relative-fibonacci-critical-correction}.  The inequalities
 372: \(2<\sigma_0<3\), already verified in
 373: \Cref{prop:critical-renewal-hypotheses}, give the stated scale comparison.
 374: \end{proof}

  25: summable domination; it does not produce the uniform higher-order remainder
  26: needed for a full corrected expansion.
  27: 
  28: At the arithmetic critical exponent, the scalar output becomes an exact
  29: regularly varying generator-cost law.  The free-generator orbit count then
  30: turns the classical Fibonacci layer sum into a renewal sum.  The tail
  31: constant \(b_C=8\) controls both the spectrally positive stable limit and the
  32: fractional \(m^{3-\sigma_0}\) term.  This is the full shared mechanism:
  33: continued-fraction contexts determine the critical tail, and renewal theory
  34: transfers that tail to the Fibonacci partition function.
  35: 
  36: The fractional term is unbounded but sublinear, hence a genuine finite-size
  37: contribution rather than a relabeling of finitely many endpoint orbits.
  38: 
  39: Several limitations follow from the proof.  The stable statement is a
  40: domain-of-attraction result for sums of generator-letter costs; it is not a
  41: local limit theorem and supplies no rate of convergence.  The Fibonacci
  42: expansion is established at the single arithmetic critical exponent and
  43: does not assert an analogous fractional correction away from criticality.
  44: Nor does the argument classify other continued-fraction weights: the exact
  45: constant uses the regular/negative continued-fraction correspondence, the
  46: two context masses, and Weinstein's particular free monoid.  Models without
  47: those identities require separate arithmetic input.

  50: starts from an exact finite-window correspondence and studies local spectra,

Implemented the correction within the requested paper directory only. The only tracked files changed are [sec_arithmetic_critical.tex](/D:/omega/automath/papers/publication/2026_brocot_condensation_critical_fibonacci_renewal_tams/sec_arithmetic_critical.tex:150) and [sec_conclusion.tex](/D:/omega/automath/papers/publication/2026_brocot_condensation_critical_fibonacci_renewal_tams/sec_conclusion.tex:28). No theorem statement, hypothesis, numbered result, mathematical constant, or structural element was changed.

**Corrected Identities**

The first corrected identity is now printed verbatim as:

```tex
\overline F^{\rm e}(j)
=\sum_{k>j}p^{\rm e}_k
=\frac1\mu\sum_{r\ge j}\overline F(r).
```

The second occurrence, in the Karamata step, is now:

```tex
\overline F^{\rm e}(j)
=\frac1\mu\sum_{r\ge j}\overline F(r)
\sim\frac{K}{\mu(\alpha-1)}j^{1-\alpha}.
```

Both appear at [sec_arithmetic_critical.tex:155](/D:/omega/automath/papers/publication/2026_brocot_condensation_critical_fibonacci_renewal_tams/sec_arithmetic_critical.tex:155) and [sec_arithmetic_critical.tex:173](/D:/omega/automath/papers/publication/2026_brocot_condensation_critical_fibonacci_renewal_tams/sec_arithmetic_critical.tex:173). A source-level check found both corrected occurrences and zero remaining `\sum_{r>j}\overline F(r)` occurrences.

The intervening sentence now explicitly records that the retained `r=j` term is

```tex
\(O(j^{-\alpha})=o(j^{1-\alpha})\)
```

and therefore does not change the discrete Karamata constant. Hence the equilibrium-tail constant remains \(K/\{\mu(\alpha-1)\}\), and after division by the additional \(\mu\), the renewal coefficient remains \(K/\{\mu^2(\alpha-1)\}\). No recalculation of the theorem constant was needed.

**Index Audit**

I checked the following boundaries and shifts:

- Checked and correct: \(p_j\) is indexed on \(j\ge1\), with \(\overline F(j)=\sum_{k>j}p_k=\Pr\{X>j\}\).

- Checked and correct: \(p_j^{\rm e}=\overline F(j-1)/\mu\) starts at \(j=1\). Its normalization uses the standard exact tail-sum identity \(\mu=\sum_{r\ge0}\overline F(r)\).

- Corrected: in \(\sum_{k>j}p_k^{\rm e}\), the range is \(k\ge j+1\); setting \(r=k-1\) gives \(r\ge j\), not \(r>j\).

- Checked and correct after correction: at \(j=0\), the equilibrium-tail formula gives \(\overline F^{\rm e}(0)=\mu^{-1}\sum_{r\ge0}\overline F(r)=1\), consistent with the equilibrium mass being supported on the positive integers.

- Checked and correct: discrete Karamata applies to \(\sum_{r\ge j}\overline F(r)\); including rather than omitting \(r=j\) changes the sum by \(O(j^{-\alpha})\), lower order than \(j^{1-\alpha}\).

- Checked and correct: the cited renewal sequence \(u_n\) in Omey–Van Gulck is mapped to the lemma’s \(v_j\) without an index shift; their tail \(\overline F_n\) maps to \(\overline F(j)\).

- Checked and correct: the generic recurrence \(v_0=1\), \(v_j=\sum_{k=1}^{j}p_kv_{j-k}\) agrees exactly with the paper’s \(u_0(\sigma_0)=1\), \(u_j(\sigma_0)=\sum_{k=1}^{j}b_k(\sigma_0)u_{j-k}(\sigma_0)\) after \(p_k=b_k(\sigma_0)\). There is no \(u_j/v_j\) shift.

- Checked and correct: \(\overline F_C(j)=\Pr\{C>j\}=\sum_{k>j}b_k(\sigma_0)\), so the critical cost tail supplies precisely the lemma’s tail convention.

- Checked and correct: \(1-\alpha=2-\sigma_0\) and \(\alpha-1=\sigma_0-2\), giving the unchanged coefficient \(K_C/\{\mu_C^2(\sigma_0-2)\}\).

- Checked and correct: the exact Fibonacci-layer identity uses \(2\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}u_j+u_m-1\). The \(j=0\) term is included exactly once in the partial renewal sum; the terminal mass is \(u_m\), not \(u_{m-1}\).

- Checked and correct: the remainder and power sums begin at \(j=1\) and end at \(m-1\). Since \(2-\sigma_0\in(-1,0)\), \(\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}j^{2-\sigma_0}\sim m^{3-\sigma_0}/(3-\sigma_0)\). The fixed \(u_0\), the distinction between \(m-1\) and \(m\), the terminal \(u_m=O(1)\), and the endpoint \(-1\) are all lower order.

- Checked and correct: in the preceding cost-tail lemma, \(2d+1>x\) is exactly equivalent to \(d>(x-1)/2\), and the termwise relation \(b_{2d+1}(s)=Z_{d+1}(s)\) uses the correct \(d+1\) shift.

\overline F^{\rm e}(j)
